It **returns** the problems it found; it never prints them, never repairs an artifact and never deletes one. `artifact_problems(artifacts, stages, root) -> dict[Path, list[str]]` is the composition function (`root` is the target root, required and defaulted nowhere, forwarded to the one check below that reads the filesystem): one `story_coordinator.read_story` call per artifact, its parse handed to `story_coordinator.stage_exception_problems` and to `strictness_problems`, keyed by path and holding only the artifacts with problems, so an empty mapping is the whole of "these may be committed". An artifact `read_story` has something to say about yields that and nothing further — `run_story`'s own pre-flight order rather than a second one, because a story that failed to parse has no parse for the later checks and one that failed its *schema* has one whose shape they may not assume (`stage_exception_problems` indexes `exception["stage"]`; `strictness_problems` splits each entry as a string).

  Five classes of problem since story-038, and the module invents the third, the fourth and the fifth. **Schema conformance** is `read_story`. **Agreement of a story's `stage_exceptions` with the loaded workflow** is `stage_exception_problems`. Neither parsing, schema validation nor the exception cross-check is reimplemented here — a plan-time check with its own reader is the divergence story-005 existed to remove — so `story_parser.parse` and `schema_validator.validate` gained no new caller. Those two are exactly what `l5-run` already refuses; moving them earlier changes nothing about pre-flight, which still refuses the same artifacts with the same status.

  The third, **`strictness_problems(story, stages)`**, runs at plan time only and is never refused by `l5-run`: adding it to pre-flight would start refusing committed artifacts that already ran. It reports a story entry that states a workflow restriction *more strictly than the workflow declares it* — a stage restricted only from creating files under a path may still modify one there, so an entry demanding it leave the path alone entirely is an unenforced rule the harness cannot see broken and one a legitimate change can make impossible to satisfy. It costs a verifier finding and a human adjudication on every story that carries it, and four committed artifacts carry one; the `planner.md` guidance against paraphrasing is the mechanism that failed all four times, which is why the check goes where the artifact is written. It scans the three free-text arrays (`acceptance_criteria`, `verification_requirements`, `constraints`), splits each entry into clauses on commas, semicolons and the standalone word `and`, and reports a clause naming a workflow-defined stage together with one of that stage's declared restricted prefixes and carrying no word scoping it to creation. Both halves come off `stage_restrictions`: grep for a stage name or a restricted prefix over `orchestration/plan_validation.py` returns nothing, docstring included, which is why the docstring writes them as `<stage>` and `<prefix>`.

  **What the third check does not catch is stated in the module docstring, where a reader meets it**, because the general problem — is this English sentence stronger than that declared rule — is not decidable and this is the narrow, stated version rather than an attempt at it. Two classes are outside it by construction. A phrasing that names *neither* the stage nor the prefix ("the stage that writes the code leaves the regression suite untouched") restricts exactly what the reported sentences restrict and matches on nothing, because both halves of the match are literals read off the workflow; any paraphrase of either half passes. And a strictness the clause split does not isolate: the split is what makes the check work at all — the historical entries confirm a file *was created* in their first clause and over-restrict a second stage in their second, so an entry-level creation-word test would let every one of them through — at the cost of the mirror case, a single clause that both restricts creation and restricts more than it ("`<stage>` neither creates nor modifies files under `<prefix>`"), which reads as scoped to creation and is not reported. It errs the other way deliberately: a clause that merely *describes* the restriction is reported if it names both halves without a creation word, because refusing a well-meant sentence costs one rephrasing at plan time, which is where a human is present to do it.

  The fourth, **`assignment_problems(story, stages, root)`**, is plan-time-only for exactly the same reason and is otherwise unlike its neighbour — which is why the module docstring now distinguishes the two rather than blending them. It reports one problem per `technical_plan.likely_file_changes` entry whose `file` falls under a prefix the entry's own `stage` is restricted from creating under, unless a grant on that stage covers the file. Since story-057 that is the whole condition: **the filesystem decides nothing about the verdict**, and an entry naming a file the target root already holds is reported exactly as one naming a file it does not. `root` is still required, and is consulted only after an entry has been decided to offend, to choose which of two fault wordings the message carries. It is called from `artifact_problems` beside `strictness_problems`, below the `read_story` gate, so the existing order is unchanged. Both halves come off `stage_restrictions` and the grant is decided by `grant_covers`, so the module still names no stage and no prefix; a story with no `technical_plan`, and an entry missing `file` or `stage`, yield nothing rather than raising. It is **structural** — two literals compared, one in the artifact and one in the workflow definition, against the story's own grants — and inherits none of the third check's hedging: no clause split, no vocabulary of scoping words, no paraphrase that evades it, nothing it declines to decide. See "Work a stage cannot own" below for why the subject is `likely_file_changes`, what the check costs, and why the judgement sits at plan time.

  The fifth, **`naming_problems(story)`**, is plan-time-only for exactly the reason the third and the fourth are — committed artifacts that have already run name their modules for story numbers, and refusing them at pre-flight would make those stories unrunnable. It reports one problem per `technical_plan.likely_file_changes` entry whose `file` has a basename matching `STORY_NUMBERED_MODULE` (`^test_story_\d+`), and the message says what to name the module instead rather than only that the name is wrong. Its subject is `likely_file_changes` for the reason `assignment_problems` takes it: that is where a plan states which files it expects written, so a name is decidable there, before anything exists under it. It is **structural** like its neighbour — one literal in the artifact against one pattern — and inherits none of the third check's hedging. It is the only one of the five that reads no workflow, so it takes `story` alone; a story with no `technical_plan`, and an entry with no `file`, yield nothing rather than raising. See "A test module is named for what it checks" below for the convention it holds and the two other mechanisms holding it.
- `plan_run_offer.py` — the mechanism behind `l5-plan`'s closing offer to run the story it just committed, added by story-059 and kept out of the script for the reason `plan_commit` and `plan_validation` are. Every function **returns what happened rather than printing it**, so `report()` stays the one place the developer-facing wording lives; nothing here prompts, and nothing here decides what the offer is worth. `can_prompt(stream)` is whether there is a terminal for the offer to be made to. Both halves of the offer ask it — the script, to decide whether to write the prompt at all, and `should_run`, to decide whether to read — so it is answered here once rather than spelled in each, because two spellings of one question are two answers that can disagree. A stream that cannot say whether it is a terminal (a closed one raises rather than answering) is treated as not one, the same one-directional bias `dirty_paths` and the Bash guard take: the cost of answering no is a command printed instead of a prompt, and the cost of answering yes wrongly is a read that never returns. `should_run(stream)` reads the reply **once and never re-asks** — an empty line runs the story, any other reply skips — and skips *without reading* on a stream `can_prompt` rejects, and on a read that reaches end of input. `run_script(harness_root)` is the `l5-run` sibling of the `l5-plan` the offer was made from, so the launch and the printed command take the executable from one place rather than each spelling it. `launch_run(harness_root, story_id, base=None)` is `subprocess.run` on that argument list and nothing else: **no `stdout`, `stderr` or capture argument is passed at all**, so the run inherits the terminal `l5-plan` was given and nothing buffers its output, and its `returncode` is returned. The working directory is left alone, because `l5-run` finds the target repository by walking up from it and that is the directory `l5-plan` was started in. `run_command(harness_root, story_id, base=None)` renders the command a skipped run would have been started with, from the same executable path, made relative to the current directory when it lies beneath it and left absolute otherwise, with a leading `./` on a relative path carrying no separator because a bare name is looked up on PATH rather than in the current directory. `--base` reaches the launch and the rendered command through one `_arguments` helper, so what is printed is what the run path would have started. The module starts nothing on its own, selects no stage, offers no editing of the artifact, and never touches the commit or the push that preceded it.
- `harness_source.py` — a **declaration the suite asserts against**, added by story-040, and the one module here that no run-time path reads. It declares where the harness's own source lives (`HARNESS_SOURCE_DIRS`: orchestration, prompts, workflows, schemas, rules, hooks, scripts, templates), which of those a target reads or describes (`TARGET_FACING_DIRS`: prompts and workflows), the tokens naming a language or toolchain (`STACK_TOKENS`), the directory shapes a target's test layout would use (`TARGET_LAYOUT_PATHS`), the one file exempt from its own scan (`EXEMPT_FILES`), and the directory names that are not source (`SKIP_DIR_NAMES`). `scan(root=None)` walks the declared directories of a repository root — this repository by default, resolved relative to the module the way `schema_validator` resolves `schemas/`, and a throwaway one built by a test otherwise, so both go through one code path — and returns sorted frozen `Finding(path, line_number, line, token, rule)` records. A directory the root does not have is skipped rather than raising, so a throwaway root need only carry what a test is about. Two rules: `STACK_RULE` reads all eight directories, `LAYOUT_RULE` reads `TARGET_FACING_DIRS` only. The coordinator does not call it, nothing routes on it, and no run is refused because of what it reports — see "No target-stack literal in harness source" below for what it is for and what it does not catch.
- `run_status.py` — read-only status snapshot backing `l5-status`. Lists every run under the configured runs directory (story id, status, current stage, retry count, sorted by story id) or shows one run's full `RunState` plus the last 10 lines of its `events.log`. The detail view prints the entry index and the run's accumulated attempt total *beside* the retry count and verification iterations rather than in place of them, because since story-062 those two counters are scoped to the entry now running and understate a resumed run when read alone; the pre-existing lines are unchanged. Reuses `story_coordinator.load_state` for state parsing (never duplicated); a run with a missing or unparseable `state.json` is flagged `unreadable` in the listing without aborting it, while the detail view fails loudly (stderr, exit 1). Never writes to run directories or anywhere else.

### Tool allowlist

Headless agents cannot answer permission prompts, so `.harness/config.yaml` carries an `allowed_tools` list of Bash command patterns (for example `Bash(.venv/bin/python:*)`) that the runner passes to every stage invocation via `--allowedTools`. A command outside the allowlist is denied, and a stage that cannot gather its evidence will fail verification honestly rather than invent it. (story-001's first execution escalated for exactly this reason before the allowlist existed.)

**Read-only breadth is granted, and it weakens no boundary.** The list carried eight prefixes and omitted every search and inspection command a stage actually reaches for; story-028's implementer was denied six calls and re-attempted the same work through narrower ones, at a turn apiece. Since story-035 it also grants `grep`, `rg`, `find`, `head`, `tail`, `wc`, `sort`, `uniq`, `diff`, `git show`, `git branch` and `git ls-files`. **The harness does not rely on this list to enforce its rules**: blocked paths, stage output ownership, the `may_not_create` prefixes and the revert check are all decided by the coordinator *after* the stage has run, from the tree and from the stage's own declarations, and `rules/execution-rules.json` lists `blocked_paths` for the coordinator to enforce rather than for the CLI to. Granting `grep` grants the ability to read faster and nothing else. Write access is a separate question and is not granted: nothing that mutates the working tree, the index or the repository is on the list, and stages have `Edit` and `Write` under `acceptEdits` and need no shell to change files. `.harness/config.yaml` is target-repository configuration, so this is *this repository's* answer; `templates/config.yaml` carries the same read-only set so an initialized repository starts where this one is, deliberately rather than by drift.

**The guard is the net behind the allowlist.** Because the widened list admits commands with mutating forms — `find -delete` is the clearest — `hooks/bash_guard.py` ships with the harness as a deny-only PreToolUse hook matching Bash, registered by `hooks/settings.json` and passed to every stage invocation through the CLI's `--settings` argument. `agent_runner` resolves the hooks directory relative to its own module, exactly as `schema_validator` resolves schemas, substitutes the guard's absolute path into the shipped declaration, and passes the result; `run_agent`'s signature is unchanged, so every fake runner the suite injects is untouched. The guard decomposes a command across pipes, semicolons, logical operators and newlines, and into the interiors of `$(...)` and backtick substitutions, then denies when a component's leading word is a mutator, when `find` is invoked with `-exec`, `-execdir`, `-delete` or `-ok`, or when a redirect names a file. It has **no allow path at all**, so a command the allowlist would refuse is never admitted by the guard reporting no problem with it, and the allowlist remains the thing that permits.

**Its bias is fail-open, deliberately.** A command it cannot parse — an unbalanced quote, an unterminated substitution, a heredoc — a malformed payload and unreadable stdin each produce *no decision* rather than a deny, and the call falls through to `allowed_tools`. A fail-closed parser mistake would stop runs that should have proceeded, which is the more expensive error, and this is the same one-directional bias `dirty_paths`, `unchanged_since_escalation` and `completion_commits` already take.

**Mode 2 is not fixed, and the prompt sentence is a cost measure rather than a boundary.** Two failure modes were visible in story-028's record. Mode 1 is an ungranted command — `grep`, `head`, `wc` — and widening the list fixes it. Mode 2 is a granted command in an ungranted *shape*: `.venv/bin/python -m pytest ... | grep -E ... > file; head -10 file` runs a granted binary, but the permission check matches the whole call string against a prefix pattern and cannot see that the leading command is permitted, so the entire call is denied. The heredoc form is the same shape. **Nothing here makes a composed call succeed.** What was added against it is a sentence in `prompts/harness-layer.md` saying each Bash call must be one command, and beside it the granted list itself, injected through `context_assembler.config_context` as `{{allowed_tools}}` from the target's own configuration so it cannot drift from what is permitted. Agents routinely ignore instructions of this kind; the story assumes only that some will follow it, and a partial hit rate on a cheap sentence is worth having. Read that text as guidance and the coordinator's post-stage checks as the enforcement — a reader who believes otherwise will narrow the guard on a false premise.

`config_context` sits beside `schema_context` and `workflow_context` and renders through the same `_dashed_lines` helper, so an absent or empty `allowed_tools` renders as `None` like every other optional placeholder. `build_context`'s new argument is **optional and keyword-only**, where `workflow` is required, and the asymmetry is deliberate rather than an inconsistency: a verifier rendered with no retry categories in it would be a defect, while a stage rendered with no granted list is exactly what every call site rendered before this existed, so a call that omits it must render what it rendered before. The coordinator passes the loaded target config's `allowed_tools` at its one `build_context` call site.

### Rules (`rules/`)

`execution-rules.json` — `max_retries`, `blocked_paths`, `require_verifier_pass`. The coordinator refuses to advance past verification without a passing `verification-result.json`, stops retrying at the ceiling, and fails a stage that modified a blocked path. **`max_retries` is here and nowhere else**: the workflow definition carried a second copy that nothing read until story-028 deleted it, and a test asserts by searching the repository — not by inspecting two known files — that exactly one definition exists. Why the ceiling belongs to the run rather than to a route is in "Where a retry goes" below. The self-route budget added by story-036 is a *different* budget, declared per stage in the workflow, and nothing in the self-route path reads or writes `max_retries` — the one definition here is untouched by it. Blocked paths are checked after every stage that declares a `changed_files` record in the workflow definition, each stage against its own record only. Stage output ownership (`may_not_create`) is checked against the same record but declared in the *workflow*, not here: blocked paths are a property of the repository and apply to every stage, while ownership is a property of one stage's role in one workflow. The revert check reads nothing from here either: it reuses the same workflow-declared prefixes and the target repository's configured `test_command`.

### Scripts (`scripts/`)

Thin entry points only; no orchestration logic. `l5-init`, `l5-plan`, `l5-run`, `l5-assist`, `l5-status`. `l5-run` takes `<story-id> [--stage <stage>] [--base <branch>]`: since story-030 both options are parsed by one small loop over the argument list rather than a positional special case, and each is passed straight through — `--stage` as `start_stage`, `--base` as the keyword-only `base` — which is the whole of the script's involvement. Validation of the stage name against the loaded workflow, and resolution and checking of the base, both happen in orchestration, because only the coordinator has loaded the workflow and only it should be deriving a base. Each resolves HARNESS_ROOT from its own location, adds `orchestration/` to `sys.path`, and locates the target repository through `harness_config.find_target_root` — one shared walk-up to the nearest `.harness/config.yaml`, not a per-script copy — before delegating to its orchestration module. (`l5-init` is the exception by design: it is *given* the directory to initialize and has nothing to find.)

`l5-plan` is the exception to "the coordinator renders prompts": the planner is not a workflow stage, so the script loads `prompts/planner.md` with `context_assembler.load_template`, renders it with `context_assembler.render`, and passes the rendered text to `--append-system-prompt`. The render context is `schema_context(HARNESS_ROOT)` merged with `workflow_context(workflow, rules)`: since story-009, `l5-plan` locates the target repository like every other run script (`find_target_root`, same no-config refusal, no session started), loads the target's config, loads the workflow the config names (default `story-workflow`) and the execution rules from HARNESS_ROOT, and injects the stage list, per-stage create restrictions, and blocked paths alongside the story schema. Requiring a target was not a loss — a planner that cannot see the project cannot list `.harness/stories/` to assign the next story number either. All lookup, loading, and rendering stays in orchestration; the script wires them together. `l5-assist` reads its template raw; sharing the render path with it is a later story.

Since story-023 the script no longer ends in `os.execvp`. It runs the session as a **child process with stdin, stdout and stderr inherited** — the developer's terminal is the session's terminal — so the script outlives the session and can commit what the session wrote. The argument list handed to `claude` is byte for byte what the exec passed, and everything before the session (target-root discovery, config loading, workflow and rules loading, the render context, the rendered planner prompt) is unchanged; the exec was replaced, not the script around it. The session's exit status becomes the script's, a child reaped with a negative return code is translated to the shell's `128 + signal`, and a `KeyboardInterrupt` in the parent is 130 — so both routes to an interrupt exit 130 and both still commit an artifact already on disk. The only new logic in the script is a `report()` function that turns `plan_commit`'s return values into stdout lines and a status; the snapshot, the commit, the push and the remote resolution live in `orchestration/plan_commit.py`, per "thin entry points only". `l5-run`, `l5-status`, `l5-assist` and `l5-init` keep the process model they had.

Outliving the session is what made **plan-time validation** possible, and story-025 put it between the two acts the script already performed. `report()` takes the loaded workflow's `stages` (which `main` already had) and calls `plan_validation.artifact_problems` after `plan_commit.new_artifacts` and before `plan_commit.commit_artifacts`, so an artifact that pre-flight would refuse is never committed. On problems it prints each artifact's problems through `story_coordinator.refuse_bad_story`, says it committed nothing and that the artifacts remain in the working tree, and returns 1 — `sys.exit(status or committing)` is unchanged, so the session's own status still wins and an interrupt still exits 130. It **refuses rather than warns**, for all three classes: a warning at plan time is a warning nobody sees, because the session that could have repaired it has ended. Nothing is committed, pushed, deleted or rewritten on that path, and no automatic repair is attempted — the developer must see the problems and decide, and the artifact stays exactly where the session wrote it, which is the state to fix and re-run from. When one of several new artifacts fails, **none** is committed: the commit is one commit for the session's artifacts and a partial one would invent a split the session did not have. The no-new-artifact early return above it is untouched — a session that wrote nothing still says it committed nothing and exits on the session's own status. The script gained no decision and no composition; both live in `plan_validation`. story-030 put a second check in the same gap, immediately below validation and still above the commit: the base check, which asks whether the branch about to receive the plan artifact is the base at all. Same shape, same refusal helper, same "committed nothing, the artifacts remain in the working tree" line — see "The base a story branches from" below. There is deliberately **no `l5-validate`** and no separate command a developer or the planner agent must remember to run: forgetting it returns the harness to the behaviour the story removed, which is also why `planner.md` gained no step telling the planner to validate its own output — the prompt paragraph is a courtesy and the script's check is the enforcement.

story-059 added an act at the other end, and it is the last thing `report()` does: an `offer()` to run the story that was just committed. It sits **after the line that prints the push and ahead of the return**, so it is reached only on the path that reports success — a commit that failed and a push that failed each return 1 above it, printing what they printed before and offering nothing. `offer()` derives the story ids from the artifact stems; with exactly one, and a terminal on stdin, it prints the prompt and reads the reply through `plan_run_offer.should_run`, returning the launched run's exit status when the answer is Enter and otherwise falling through to the printed command. With more than one it prints the command for each and offers nothing, because the offer is run-or-skip for one story and a session that wrote several gave no order to choose from. **When stdin is not a terminal the offer is not made at all and the skip path is taken**: nothing is written, nothing is read, the command is printed exactly as it would have been had the developer declined, and a scripted or cron invocation exits with the status it would have had rather than blocking on a prompt nothing can answer. That is also why there is deliberately **no `--yes` and no `--no-run` flag** — the terminal check already answers the non-interactive case, and a flag would be a second answer to it, the shape "One act, one mechanism" warns against below. What is committed and pushed, and when, is unchanged, and the offer never unwinds or re-reports either: the worst it does is print a command. The script gained no decision — `plan_run_offer` holds it, per "thin entry points only" — and `scripts/l5-run` was not touched.

### Target-repository state (`.harness/`)

- `config.yaml` — repository-specific settings (branch prefix, model, permission mode, workflow name, `test_command`, and `verification_runner`), plus the optional `base_branch` key story-030 added, shipped as a commented example. The keys the harness reads are declared in `schemas/harness-config.schema.json` and each is proven to govern (see the `schemas/` section); the file itself is validated against nothing at run time, except that a retired key refuses the run (see the retired-key paragraph in the `schemas/` section). Leaving `base_branch` unset is the normal case: the base is then the repository's own default branch, read from `refs/remotes/origin/HEAD`. Set it only where the base is not the git default.
- `standards/` — repository standards (architecture, coding, testing) that verifiers evaluate against.
- `stories/` — approved story artifacts produced by `l5-plan`, and validated, committed and pushed by it (see the `plan_validation.py` and `plan_commit.py` bullets above). The directory is what `snapshot` watches; its location comes from the config's `stories_dir`, defaulting to `.harness/stories`.
- `runs/<story-id>/` — per-run state, events, and artifacts (not committed).
- `logs/` — raw agent output logs (not committed).

## Story lifecycle

    Approved story artifact (.harness/stories/story-NNN.yaml)
        ↓
    l5-run → Story Coordinator
        ↓
    implement → test → document → verify ──fail──→ retry the stage the reported
        ↓ pass                              ↑        category routes to (bounded)
        │                                   │        ↓ retries exhausted, or a
    clean-clone check ──fail────────────────┘          verdict that cannot be routed
        ↓ pass                                       escalated (escalation-summary.md)
        ↓
    completed (completion-report.md)

Any stage in that sequence may also fail **mechanically** — its agent process
dies, or it does not write a required output, or the output at the run root is
a previous attempt's. That is not a verdict and routes nowhere: the stage runs
again in place, bounded by its own `max_self_routes` and spending none of the
retry budget above. A stage declaring no budget escalates instead, which no
stage of this workflow does since story-060. See "A stage that failed
mechanically runs again".

Since story-066 one route into that same in-place re-run is not a mechanical
failure at all: a stage may declare a `suite_run`, and the coordinator runs the
target's configured test command as a subprocess after that stage's turn ends.
A zero exit advances; a non-zero exit brings the stage back in place on the
same budget, spending no retry. The shipped workflow declares it on the tester.
See "The coordinator runs the suite a stage declares".

An escalation is not the end of the run. `l5-run <story-id>` on a run whose
`state.json` says `escalated` **resumes** it at the recorded stage; only
`completed` still refuses.

    escalated ──l5-run [--stage <stage>]──→ running, at the recorded stage
        │                                        (or the one --stage names)
        ├──unchanged story, tree and harness──→ refused
        └──uncommitted work in the target tree─→ refused

A `completed` run refuses on its recorded state. Since story-027 a run whose
*branch* carries the story's completion commit refuses too, whether or not the
run directory is still there — deleting the directory no longer makes a
finished story runnable again.

## Resuming a run

Chapter 18 treats a crash and an escalation as the same problem, and its recovery pseudocode branches on both statuses identically: restore the artifacts, set `current_stage` from the recorded state, continue. `run_story` already did that for a `running` run; story-020 narrowed the already-ended refusal so it fires on `completed` alone and let an `escalated` run take the same path. Nothing was added to the resume mechanism — the artifacts and the state are already on disk, which is exactly what Chapter 18 names as the mechanism, and is why `.harness/requests/README.md`'s decision not to write checkpoints still stands after this story rather than being reopened by it.

The resume branch runs in this order, and the order is the point: evaluate the unchanged guard, set the stage, refuse a collision, archive the interrupted attempt, move the ending entry's counter-keyed artifacts, zero the counters, then set `status` back to `running` and save, then append the events. A guard that ran after the state was rewritten would be deciding about a run that no longer exists; the archive numbers its attempt from the counters as they stood, so it must precede the reset; and both refusals are decided above everything they protect, so a refused resume archives nothing, moves nothing and resets nothing.

**Most of those acts are the same for both resumed statuses; two are not.** Setting the stage, the collision refusals, archiving the interrupted attempt, moving the ending entry's counter-keyed artifacts and zeroing the counters run for a `running` run and an `escalated` one alike. The state transition and the unchanged guard are `escalated`-only, and each for its own reason: a crashed run is already `running`, so assigning that status would state a transition that did not happen, and a crashed run has no escalation commit to compare against, so `unchanged_since_escalation` could establish nothing and its refusal would be meaningless. Story-020 wrote the archive inside the escalated branch, which left the asymmetry running the wrong way round — an escalated run reached a decision the harness made deliberately and recorded, while a crashed run stopped for a reason nobody recorded, which is when a partial artifact is most likely to be the only account of what happened. story-061 hoisted the archive out; the transition and the guard stayed where they were.

**A resume restores the run's attempt allowance.** `retry_count` and `verification_iterations` both go to zero, so a run that escalated at the retry ceiling resumes with its attempts back rather than escalating again on the first failing verdict — which was a second escalation rather than a resume. The rule the reset conforms to is Chapter 18's: a counter routes and a record accounts; a counter is reset by the decision to continue, and a record is never reset at all. Until story-062 the counters were carried forward, and the comment that justified it argued from filenames rather than from policy — the two counters key `prompt-<stage>-attempt-N.md` and `verification/iteration-N.json`, so resetting them alone would have written over the earlier attempts' evidence.

**So the run is re-entered rather than restarted.** Re-entry K opens `resumes/resume-K/` and moves every counter-keyed name at the run root into it, each keeping its position beneath the run directory, and only then are the counters zeroed. `attempt-N` therefore keeps meaning the Nth attempt of *one entry* — Chapter 17's argument against a shared self-route budget ("a retry count of two should mean two verification failures, not one forgotten file and one") applied to a case the book does not apply it to. A monotonic attempt index that never reset would satisfy "records are never reset" equally well and **was rejected for that reason alone**: it splits the number that names files from the number checked against `max_retries`, and every site deriving `state.retry_count + 1` would then have to say which it meant. `state.retry_count + 1` stays the one expression that numbers an attempt.

Three helpers hold the mechanism, and none of them writes a stage name or an artifact name:

- `entry_dir(run_dir, entry)` is the one place `resumes/resume-K/` is named, in `attempt_dir`'s shape and for its reason: the move that writes it and the refusal that guards it both derive it from there, so the two cannot disagree about where an entry's evidence went.
- `entry_artifacts(run_dir, stages)` collects the run-root names a reset would re-land on: the attempt-numbered prompts, the try-suffixed prompts and `self-route-*` records those attempts wrote, the `verification/iteration-*.json` files and the `attempts/` directory. The prompt and self-route names are discovered through `prompt_file` and `self_route_result_file` with the attempt and the try number wildcarded — the idiom `self_route_artifacts` already uses — so discovery cannot drift from writing; the stage names come off the loaded workflow and the attempts directory off `attempt_dir`. Names are run-relative.
- `move_entry_artifacts(run_dir, names, destination)` moves each name preserving that position, so `verification/iteration-1.json` lands at the same relative path inside the entry rather than beside it. It makes the same claim `archive_attempt` makes about what it copies, which is none: a truncated file a dying stage left is moved exactly as it is, and nothing on this path parses, validates or repairs a moved artifact.

**Story-063 added a third counter to the reset and a fourth record to the root, and neither is an exception to the rule above.** `entry_cost_usd` — the entry's cost allowance — goes to zero beside `retry_count` and `verification_iterations`, for the reason they do: it is policy, and a human typing `l5-run` is the authorization to spend another allowance. `stopped_on_cost` is cleared with it. `cost.json` stays at the run root and continues from where it stood, so a resumed run's record still accounts for the invocations of every earlier entry. The resume appends a note stating both halves — the recorded total across the run, the entry's own spend before the reset, and, when the run stopped on a ceiling, that resuming funds another allowance — which is what makes the reset a decision rather than an accident. See "A run has a cost ceiling" below.

**What stays at the run-directory root is the half that accounts for the whole run**, and nothing moves it: `retry-history.json`, `cost.json`, `execution-history.json`, `events.log`, `state.json` and `escalation-summary.md`. Their contents are not rewritten either, on the move or after it. Every stage artifact the resumed stage reads as input also stays — those carry canonical names no counter keys, so a resume at a later stage assembles exactly the context it assembled before. The correction-pass records stay too: `correction_pass_count` is cumulative across the run and is *not* reset by a resume — the budget is one pass per run and a resumed run must not spend it twice — so its names cannot collide and have no reason to move.

**One event records what moved and where.** Appended through the existing `append_event` path, it names the entry directory and every name that went into it, both run-relative. That is what makes a retry record's `archive_directory`, written before the move and now one directory further down, resolvable from the log alone — a record is evidence, evidence is never rewritten, and the log is what resolves it.

**A resume onto an existing `resumes/resume-K/` is a refusal**, in the shape story-061's archive-collision refusal established and naming the directory, the story and the entry. Both refusals are decided above the archive and the move, so a refused resume leaves the run exactly as it found it.

**`state.resume_count` is a field on `state.json`, not a count of directories on disk.** The coordinator routes from state and `state.json` is the record of what is true now; the book states that principle repeatedly and never addresses scanning a filesystem, so this is our application of it rather than its text. It is defaulted like every field beside it, so a state file written before story-062 loads and reads as the first entry — a run escalated before this story resumes rather than failing to parse.

Two riders come with the reset, both in the escalation summary's Recommended Investigation section and both facts every escalation has, so neither branches on a reason's text. `accumulated_attempts(run_dir, state)` composes the run's total from `resume_count` and `load_retry_records` rather than from the live counters, which are now entry-scoped, and the section states that total and says that resuming restores the allowance — a developer decides about a ceiling escalation with the accumulated cost in front of them, and with the distinction between "stopped converging" and "stopped inside its allowance" said outright. No escalation reason string changed, and the summary's heading, `## Status`, `## Reason`, `## Where Execution Stopped` and `## Where to Look` are byte for byte what they were.

A **re-run** — deleting the run directory and running the story again — begins with no state, no entry directory and zeroed counters, so it agrees with all of this by construction and nothing about it changed.

**`self_route_count` was the first field a resume zeroed, and story-062 made the other two agree with it rather than the reverse.** It is live state scoped to the stage currently running, not a cumulative record — the history holds the record — so a resumed stage starts with its full budget, exactly as a stage entered by advancing or by a retry's reroute does. The evidence is still preserved: because the count is zeroed, a resumed stage's first mechanical failure is try 1 of the same attempt again and would write over the names the interrupted attempt's own self-routes used, so `interrupted_attempt_artifacts` archives those too. It takes an optional `run_dir` for this and reads them off the directory with `self_route_artifacts`, since names keyed by a discarded counter cannot be derived from the workflow alone.

**The interrupted attempt is archived before the resumed stage runs**, under the attempt number it was written with (`state.retry_count + 1`, the same expression the retry branch uses). `interrupted_attempt_artifacts(stages, attempt, *, run_dir=None)` is `archivable_artifacts(stages)` plus that attempt's rendered prompt filenames, one per stage, plus — when a run directory is given — whatever that attempt's self-routes left behind — the prompts are the addition a resume needs, because a run re-entering under the same attempt number re-renders over the prompt the interrupted stage was actually given. Both lists come off the loaded workflow; no stage name and no artifact name is written in orchestration. An archive directory that already exists is a refusal, not an overwrite (see the story-010 bullet below), and the refusal is **one expression** reached by both resumed statuses rather than a copy per path, so the two resumes cannot drift into refusing differently.

The archive belongs on the crashed path as much as the escalated one because a re-entered run re-renders each stage's prompt under a counter-keyed name unconditionally, and the dying attempt's own artifacts sit at the run root under names the resumed run will claim. Without the archive the interrupted attempt's rendered prompt is simply replaced. With it, both copies exist and differ: the archived prompt is the file the interrupted stage was actually given, and the run root holds the resumed stage's fresh rendering. **The archive runs above the move and is unchanged by it** — it still numbers its attempt from `state.retry_count + 1` as the counters stood before the reset, and its output then travels into `resumes/resume-K/attempts/` with the rest of `attempts/`, which is where a reader finds it after a resume. Before story-062 the two prompts differed at the same name in different directories; now the archived one is under the entry directory at the attempt number the interrupted attempt used, and the fresh rendering is at the run root under the attempt-1 name the restored allowance gives it.

**Archiving makes no claim about what it copied.** A stage that died mid-write may leave a truncated or schema-invalid file; preserving it exactly as it is is the point, and nothing on this path parses, validates or repairs an archived artifact. Nor is the archive conditional on what the dying stage left behind: an artifact the interrupted attempt never wrote is skipped rather than treated as an error, exactly as `archive_attempt` already skips an absent conditional artifact, and an attempt that wrote nothing still produces the directory — and so still refuses a second resume.

The dirty-tree pre-flight still exempts a `running` state, and it and the archive do not interact: the pre-flight reads the working tree, the archive touches only the run directory.

**Resuming is inferred from the recorded status and from nothing else.** The guard adds only a refusal, for the one case where a resume is knowably pointless. `unchanged_since_escalation(state, story_text, target_root, harness_root)` makes three comparisons, each of which must be *establishable* before it says anything: the story artifact's SHA-256 against `state.story_digest`, the branch against `state.escalation_commit` with a clean `git status --porcelain`, and the harness against what the state recorded of it. It returns the evidence only when all three are establishable and identical, and an empty list otherwise — **anything it cannot establish counts as not-the-same**, so an absent digest, an escalation that committed nothing, an unreadable HEAD, or a harness root that is not a git repository produces no refusal rather than a false one. Since story-034 the third comparison has **two forms** and still counts as one comparison — see "When the harness is its own target" below. The refusal message names the escalation reason (read from `escalation-summary.md` by `escalation_reason`, for the message only — nothing routes on it) and says what to change: amend the story, change the code on the branch, or update the harness. Amending the story clears it. The digest informs that message; it never authorizes or triggers a resume, so an incidental edit to a story cannot silently restart a run.

One resumed event is appended through the existing `append_event` path, naming the stage, so the routing stays reconstructable from the log alone and `events.log` and `execution-history.json` stay two renderings of one write.

### When the harness is its own target

The guard was reasoned about for a **separate-repository deployment**, where a run touches the target and never the harness checkout, so the recorded revision is a fact the run cannot move and the third comparison compares what it was written to compare. This repository is the other configuration — the harness *is* the target — and there the recorded-revision comparison could never say "unchanged". `state.harness_revision` is written as the first act of `_escalate`, before `commit_escalated_work` and `commit_escalated_tree` move HEAD, so a shared-root resume always read a pre-escalation revision against a post-escalation one. The one shape where HEAD does not move — an escalation with a clean tree, which commits nothing — is already ended earlier by the empty `escalation_commit`. There was therefore **no path on which the guard could refuse in this repository**, and story-028's resume happened for that reason rather than because anything about it had changed. This is drift into a configuration the guard was not reasoned about in, not a design error in it.

`same_repository(target_root, harness_root)` decides the case, from `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` in each root through the existing `_git` helper, reporting shared only when **both** invocations succeed and their outputs are equal. A failing invocation on either side returns False — the not-established answer — which is the same one-directional bias `_revision`, `dirty_paths`, `completion_commits` and `base_problems` take, so a root that is not a git repository falls through to the revision comparison rather than producing a false refusal.

**Under a shared root the branch comparison stands for the harness one**, and the deferral is sound only *because* leg two ran first: it has established that the branch is exactly the escalation commit with nothing uncommitted, and under one tree that covers every change to the harness source. The third comparison's evidence line then names the shared checkout instead of a recorded revision — "the harness is the same checkout as the target, so it is covered by the branch comparison above". The shared-root branch therefore sits *after* leg two and could not sit before it; any earlier and it would assert something not yet established. Under separate roots the `state.harness_revision` comparison and its evidence line are unchanged character for character, so a separate-repository deployment reads exactly as it did.

**A tree hash over the harness's own source directories was considered and rejected.** It is a second definition of "has the harness changed" living beside the branch comparison that already answers it, and it would have to choose which directories count — a choice nothing else in the harness makes. **Moving where the revision is recorded was not attempted either**, and the reasoning should not be re-derived: any revision recorded before the escalation's commits is stale afterwards, and any recorded after cannot be inside the commit that records it — the same circularity the two-commit shape already solves for `escalation_commit`. Both rejections are written at the comparison itself as well as here.

Nothing about the structure moved. The function still makes exactly **three** comparisons, still returns evidence only when all three are establishable and identical, and still refuses only on positive evidence; the third simply has two forms. `_escalate`'s assignment of `state.harness_revision`, both escalation commits, `_revision` and `_resume_refusal`'s guidance sentence are untouched, and **no flag, environment variable or configuration key** that skips or weakens the guard exists.

**One recorded run state now skips the guard, and it skips it at the call site rather than weakening it.** Since story-063 a run carrying `state.stopped_on_cost` — one stopped because a cost ceiling was reached rather than because the work failed — is not put to the guard at all. The guard would establish exactly what it always establishes and would be right about it: the story, the branch and the harness are all unchanged, because a budget stop is not a judgement about the work and nothing needs to have changed for the resume to be useful. Resuming such a run is the decision to fund another allowance, which is the response the ceiling exists to permit. Deciding that above the guard keeps `unchanged_since_escalation` saying only what it can establish, rather than teaching it a reason it would have to read to answer.

What changes for a developer working in this repository: a story-028-shaped escalation — clean tree, story unamended, no new commit — **stops resuming and starts refusing**. `--stage` is the intended override, already exists and is already named in the refusal message, so nothing new is needed, but the working habit changes. The escalation summary's standing promise, that the resume is refused while the story artifact, the branch and the harness are all unchanged, becomes true in this repository for the first time.

### The escalation commit

Resume cannot recover what the harness did not preserve, which is why the commit is part of this story rather than a neighbour of it. `_complete` ended a successful run with `git add -A` and a commit; `_escalate` committed nothing, so the one terminal state a developer most needs to inspect, resume or hand to another agent was the one left in the working tree, surviving exactly until someone checked out another branch — a normal thing to do while deciding what to do about an escalation. story-018's escalation left seven files and 1174 insertions uncommitted on `story/story-018`, preserved by a hand-written commit (`e2f401d`) the harness should have made.

An escalation now leaves **two** commits, and the second is not bookkeeping for its own sake:

1. `commit_escalated_work(target_root, state, reason, run_dir=...)` commits the run's own record of the escalation — `state.json` and both renderings of the event stream — and returns its sha, which is what `state.escalation_commit` records. It returns `""` on a clean tree, and then nothing further is committed: an escalation with nothing to commit records no commit and is not an error.
2. `commit_escalated_tree`, invoked by the `_commits_the_tree_it_ends_on` decorator on `_escalate` after `_escalate` has written everything including the summary, runs `git add -A` and commits the work on top. That commit is the branch tip.

Two commits rather than one because **a commit cannot carry its own sha**: the content is hashed into the identity being recorded. In a repository that tracks its run directory, a clean tree requires the committed `state.json` to equal the on-disk one, and the on-disk one records the sha of the commit containing it — a fixed point of the hash over its own tree. Splitting the record from the work is what lets `state.json` be both committed and carry a real sha. Both commits use `--allow-empty`, so the branch shape is the same in a repository that ignores its run directory as in one that tracks it, which is what lets one undo command be right in both. The decorator exists to put the work commit *last* without moving `_escalate`'s own writing: the escalation summary's construction and write stay byte for byte what they were, which the "do not change `escalation-summary.md`'s content" constraint required.

Consequences worth knowing: `ESCALATION_UNDO_COMMAND` is `git reset --mixed HEAD~2`, named in the commit body, and `unchanged_since_escalation` compares the recorded commit against `HEAD~1` rather than `HEAD`, because the escalation commit is the tip's parent.

The subject is `l5 escalated: <story-id> stopped at <stage>`, built from `ESCALATION_COMMIT_MARKER`. `_complete`'s is `<story-id>: <title>`, so a subject leading with a marker cannot be read as a completion in `git log --oneline`, in a PR title, or by anyone scanning the branch. The body says outright that the commit is a holding place rather than a decision about the work, carries the escalation reason, and names the undo command. `_complete` is untouched — same message, same contents, same behaviour.

### The escalation summary

`escalation-summary.md` is the terminal escalation report, and since story-024 it **carries the finding rather than a pointer to it**. The standard it is held to is Chapter 17's: a developer needs enough to understand what happened without reconstructing the execution history by hand. story-009's summary said "verification failed and the verifier did not recommend a retry" and named a directory; which criterion was unsatisfiable, which files carried the duplicated code, and why no retry in scope could fix it were all on disk in `verification-result.json`, and reaching them required knowing that file exists.

The composition is a module-level `escalation_summary(run_dir, state, reason) -> str`, so a report can be rendered against a hand-built run directory without driving a run. `_escalate` calls it where it used to build the f-string and writes the result to the same path; the escalation's ordering — state saved, event appended, record commit made, summary written, tree commit made by the decorator — is unchanged, and so is the decorator above it.

Eight sections, in this order, each rendering an artifact that already exists:

| Section | Rendered from | Present when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| heading, `## Status`, `## Reason`, `## Where Execution Stopped` | `RunState` and the escalation reason | always, byte for byte what they were before story-024 |
| `## Outstanding Issues` | `verification-result.json` | that artifact is present, parseable, and records `failed` |
| `## Retry History` | `retry-history.json`, through the existing `load_retry_records` | that reader returns entries, i.e. the run retried — an absent artifact and an artifact holding no entries are the same case |
| `## Where to Look` | — | always, unchanged |
| `## Recommended Investigation` | the run directory's own contents, `state.branch`, `state.escalation_commit`, `state.story_id`, `ESCALATION_UNDO_COMMAND` | always |

Four properties are load-bearing:

- **A section whose source is absent is omitted entirely** — no heading, no placeholder. An empty section under a heading is the failure mode the story exists to remove, not an acceptable degradation, and it is what an assertion should be written against: read the produced text and assert the heading string does not appear, rather than asserting a section is empty. A missing-artifact or blocked-path escalation has no verdict behind it and a run that never retried has no history; both are read off the artifact's own absence. An artifact that is present but unparseable is treated as absent, because a report is not where an unreadable artifact should raise.
- **The coordinator renders, never summarizes.** Outstanding Issues and Retry History reproduce each blocking issue's `severity`, `issue`, `location` and `required_behavior` as the verifier recorded them — not a count, not a paraphrase, not a pointer. Outstanding Issues opens by naming both `verification-result.json` and the `verification/iteration-N.json` matching `state.verification_iterations`, because the verdict may predate the stage the run escalated at and a reader has to be able to tell.
- **Recommended Investigation branches on facts, never on the reason's text.** It lists the run-directory files that exist, states that the work is committed and names `ESCALATION_UNDO_COMMAND` *only* when `state.escalation_commit` is non-empty — an escalation with a clean tree records no commit and the section then claims none — and says that `l5-run <story-id>` resumes at the recorded stage, that `--stage` overrides it, and that the resume is refused while story, branch and harness are all unchanged. Since story-062 it also states the attempts the run has accumulated across every entry of it, from `accumulated_attempts`, and that resuming restores the attempt allowance; both hold of every escalation, so neither reads a reason. Since story-063 it states what the run has cost on the same terms and for the same reason — the total from `cost.json`, which accounts for every entry, and the current entry's spend from `state.entry_cost_usd`, which is what a run ceiling is compared against and what resuming returns to zero. `completion-report.md` gains its own `## Cost` section stating the recorded total, so both terminal reports say what the run cost. The added sentences avoid the word *committed*, which the clean-tree case must not claim. Classifying the escalation reason would be the coordinator reasoning about a fact rather than reporting it.
- **`## Reason` stays immediately after `## Status`.** `escalation_reason` reads the summary by splitting on `## Reason` and truncating at the next `##`, for a refusal message only; nothing else reads the file and nothing routes on it. Keeping the two adjacent is what makes that reader return the same string it returned before the new sections existed.

The story added **no file and no schema**: every section renders something already on disk, and the run directory and `schemas/manifest.json` are unchanged. `retry-history.json` keeps its one reader in orchestration — the summary calls `load_retry_records` rather than reading the file again — which is also why `tests/test_retry_history.py`'s "nothing routes on the retry history" scan exempts the renderer by name: rendering a recorded fact into a human report is not a routing decision. `_complete` and `completion-report.md` are untouched; the success-path counterpart is a separate concern.

## The tree a run starts from

A run commits the tree it ends on, so it has to start from a tree it can account for. Both terminal commits stage with `git add -A`, and story-021 made that an **accurate statement about what the run produced** without changing either commit: the accuracy is established at the start, not enforced at the end. Staging less would have been the regression — a commit assembled from the paths the stage records name is a tree no stage validated, and it silently drops a real change no record happened to name. The tree the verifier verified is the tree that should be committed; the defect was that the verifier was verifying a tree that already held the developer's unrelated work, which was therefore never checked either and was carried through the whole run.

Observed, not hypothetical. story-013's commit contains `.harness/stories/story-013.yaml`, 237 lines, visible in `.harness/runs-archive/story-013-vacuous-tests/pre-reset-branch.patch`. No stage produced it — `l5-plan` wrote it before the run began and left it uncommitted, and `git add -A` absorbed it — and the run's own record of what changed names four files and does not include it. Because the artifact went into the story commit rather than onto the base branch, discarding the branch for the re-run would have destroyed the only copy of the story's definition, and it had to be recovered from the archive.

**Which runs the check applies to is decided by the state the run is starting from**, and that state is the one the resume decision already loads:

- **No state — a fresh run.** It applies. Nothing uncommitted can be this run's work, because the run has not started.
- **`escalated` — a resume of an escalated run.** It applies, and this is the case the two requests were planned together to settle. The escalation commit above leaves the tree clean, so anything uncommitted when the developer resumes is the developer's own fix, and committing it deliberately is exactly what the check is for.
- **`running` — a resume of a crashed run.** It does **not** apply, and this is not so much an exception to the rule as a different tree. Nothing commits when a process dies, so that working tree holds the run's own unfinished work; refusing it would refuse the run its own state.
- **`completed`** is unreachable by the check — the already-ended refusal sits below it and is untouched.

The check sits above the run-directory creation, the log path, the completed-run refusal, the resume branch and `_checkout_story_branch`, so a refusal leaves **no run directory, no `state.json`, no log, no new branch, and invokes no agent**. The message names every dirty path and says `Commit or stash them, then run the story again.` — the friction is the point of contact a developer meets most often. There is deliberately **no flag, environment variable or configuration key** that skips it: a bypass on a correctness guard becomes the default invocation, and a check that warns and proceeds is what the harness effectively had when story-013's artifact went by unnoticed.

**The story artifact's path is not exempt.** `l5-plan` commits and pushes the artifact it wrote (story-023), so the exemption would buy nothing, and it would leave the artifact where story-013 showed it to be fragile — single-copy on a branch that may be discarded. An uncommitted artifact therefore refuses the run and is named like any other path.

**The three pre-flight guards on an escalated resume say three different things and overlap nowhere**, which is the property to keep when any of them is edited:

| Tree state on an escalated resume | Outcome |
| --- | --- |
| Clean, story and harness unchanged since the escalation | Refused by `unchanged_since_escalation` — the resume would repeat itself |
| Dirty | Refused by the clean-tree check, naming the paths |
| Clean, with a new commit on the branch | Resumes |

**The guarantee has exactly one gap**, and it is this check's one exclusion rather than a looseness in either commit: a resumed *crashed* run can stage more than it produced. That limit is stated where the commits are made and driven by a test that goes red when it stops being true, which is the same shape story-020 used for the limit this story closed.

## The branch a run starts from

A run's `state.json` says whether *this* run directory ended; the branch says whether the *story* did, and until story-027 only the first was consulted. `_checkout_story_branch` reuses an existing story branch rather than resetting it, so deleting a finished run's directory and running the story again checked out the branch still carrying the completed work. Every stage then did its job correctly against it: the implementer opened a repository where the story was already done, the tester found the tests written and passing, the verifier verified a tree that genuinely satisfies the story, and the run completed. The output was a green run that changed nothing, with nothing in the run directory distinguishing it from a real one. `4d8cdf3` made the existing already-ended message name the branch; story-027 makes the coordinator enforce what that message describes.

**The evidence is a finished run, not the existence of commits**, and the distinction is the whole design rather than a nicety. `completion_commits` reports a commit only when *two* things hold together: some line of its message names **this** story in `completion_commit_subject(story_id, title)`'s shape, and its message carries `COMPLETION_COMMIT_MARKER` — the sentence `_complete` writes and nothing else does. Neither half alone would do. `<story-id>: <title>` is a shape any hand-written commit about the story can wear, so refusing on the subject would refuse work the harness never did; the marker without the subject would report another story's run. Both are required *anywhere in the message*, subject and body together, rather than in particular fields of it; until story-065 the subject shape was matched against `%s` alone, which no squash-merged completion can satisfy. See "The completion commit survives any merge" below.

**The `git rev-list <base>..<branch> --count` test the request proposed was not adopted**, and the reasoning against it should not be re-derived. It rested on the coordinator only ever committing in `_complete`, which expired with story-020: an escalation now leaves two commits, so a branch ahead of its base is no longer the same thing as a branch whose run finished, and refusing on it would refuse every escalated resume. The check is also **base-free** for a second reason — it still holds when the developer is standing on the story branch a completed run left them on, where a base-relative test has no base to name. And it is stated as a fact about *finished work* rather than about commits existing so that a later design committing partial progress is not forced to unpick it. That reasoning is written at the check itself and in `completion_commits`'s docstring, not only here, and an assertion holds it there.

**The composition and the recognition are one fact.** `completion_commit_message(state, title)` was extracted out of `_complete`'s inline f-string — the only edit to `_complete`, with the committed bytes unchanged — and both it and `completion_commits` go through `completion_commit_subject`, so the pre-flight matches the prefix the composition builds rather than a second spelling of the same shape. The same reasoning as `refuse` being shared between plan time and pre-flight: two definitions that agree today are two definitions that can drift. `story_branch(config, story_id)` is the same move for the branch name, now derived once and used by both the fresh run's `RunState` and the new check; `branch_prefix` appears exactly once in the coordinator, and no branch name, default base branch name or story id is written into the new code.

**It refuses only on positive evidence**, matching the bias `dirty_paths` and `unchanged_since_escalation` already take: a branch that does not exist, a root that is not a git repository, and a failing git invocation each report nothing rather than a partial verdict. A story branch that exists but carries no completion commit still runs — a branch created and then abandoned before `_complete` is a normal state, not an error.

**The coordinator resets nothing and deletes nothing.** Discarding a completed run is the developer's decision, and a coordinator that silently reset a branch would be a worse defect than the one this closes. The refusal names the branch, each commit found, and the run directory, and says what to do about each: a completion commit reachable from the branch you plan to run from means the story has already shipped, so a new story is the right response rather than a reset; resetting or deleting the branch discards the finished work; the run directory is gitignored and will not come back; archive either to `.harness/runs-archive/` first if it is worth keeping. The target repository is only read — no commit, no branch, no checkout, no index change, no stash.

**A `completed` state keeps its own refusal.** The new check is skipped when the loaded state records `completed`, byte for byte the message that case produces today: that one has the run directory to point at and says more. The two overlap in outcome and not in message, and which one a developer meets is decided by whether the run directory survived.

**The five pre-flight guards now say five different things**, which is the property to keep when any of them is edited:

| State a run starts from | Outcome |
| --- | --- |
| The branch carries this story's completion commit | Refused by `completion_commits`, naming the commits |
| `state.json` records `completed` | Refused by the already-ended message, naming the run directory |
| The story branch does not exist and HEAD is not on the base, or the base has drifted from its remote | Refused by `base_problems`, naming the base |
| Dirty tree (fresh run or escalated resume) | Refused by the clean-tree check, naming the paths |
| Escalated, clean, story and harness unchanged | Refused by `unchanged_since_escalation` — the resume would repeat itself |

The base check is third in the table because it is third in the code, above the clean-tree check: a developer standing on the wrong branch is told that before being told to commit or stash work they may not want on this branch at all.

## The completion commit survives any merge

A completion commit that only exists on the story branch is a completion commit the trunk never learns about, and until story-065 that was the normal outcome for any run that escalated. The escalation commits the whole working tree; a resumed entry that reaches `_complete` has therefore already committed everything, so `git add -A` stages nothing and the completion is the **empty** commit `--allow-empty` produces. A merge commit keeps it, a rebase drops it, and a squash folds it away. `origin/main` carries the evidence: `947ec61` and `3346a28` are the escalation commits of story-061 and story-063, and no commit above either says the story finished, while story-062 — which never escalated, so its completion carried the work — landed as `fd167f3` with its completion intact.

**`_complete` now has three outcomes rather than one**, decided by what staging left and by what the branch tip says about itself:

| After `git add -A` | What is committed |
| --- | --- |
| Something staged | `completion_commit_message(state, title)`, `--allow-empty`, a new commit — byte for byte what it always did |
| Nothing staged, and HEAD is an escalation commit of *this* story | `git commit --amend --allow-empty` with `completion_commit_message(state, title, amended=True)` |
| Nothing staged, anywhere else | The `--allow-empty` commit, unchanged |

The middle case is the repair. The trunk then sees **one** commit that both carries the work the escalation committed and says the story finished, and every merge method preserves it: a merge commit keeps it, a rebase has a non-empty commit to replay, and a squash folds its message into the body where the reader now looks.

**The amend is confined to that one shape, and the confinement is the point.** The tree must be clean and HEAD must be an escalation commit of the story being run. `_head_escalated(target_root)` reads `git log -1 --format=%s` and puts it to `escalated_story` — recognition by what the commit says about itself, never by counting, position or ordering — and `_complete` amends only when the answer equals `state.story_id`. Another story's escalation commit is never rewritten. `_head_escalated` answers `None` for an unreadable HEAD, a root that is not a git repository and any other subject shape, the same one-directional bias `completion_commits` and `dirty_paths` take: answering `None` costs the empty commit the harness already wrote, and a false yes costs a rewritten commit that was not this run's.

**Amending destroys the only trunk-visible record of the escalation, so the replacement message has to carry it.** `AMENDED_COMPLETION_NOTE` is appended *below* the marker: the run escalated, this commit is that escalation's amended rather than added to, it carries the whole working tree the escalation committed, and `ESCALATION_UNDO_COMMAND` no longer applies to it — interpolated from that constant rather than respelled, so the two move together. The subject line and the marker sentence are byte-identical in both forms, so the amended commit is recognised by exactly what recognises every other completion, and there is still one composition of each.

**The reader stopped caring which field its evidence sits in.** `completion_commits` joins `%s` and `%b` and asks both questions of the whole message; `_carries_completion_subject` strips a leading `* ` or `- ` (`MERGE_BULLETS`) before comparing a line against `completion_commit_subject(story_id, "")`, because that is how a squash writes a folded subject into the body. Nothing else about the line is tolerated, so a commit that merely mentions the story is not a completion, and both pieces are still required *together*. Each reported line still carries the commit's **real** subject rather than the line that matched — for a squashed completion that is the pull request's title, which is what a reader scanning the trunk sees.

`commit_escalated_work` and `commit_escalated_tree` are unchanged, and `tests/history-fixtures/story_coordinator.*.at-story-065-baseline.py.txt` holds them to it. The escalation's two-commit shape and its capture of the whole working tree are load-bearing — see "The escalation commit" above — and the empty completion was a consequence of that being right, not a defect in it. `conftest.story_commit_range` needed no change either: it resolves an escalated story's endpoint through `completion_commits` rather than by ordering, so the amended tip is found by the same recognition, and `tests/conftest.py`'s `escalating_story` gained an `amended=` shape beside the one it already built.

**What a squash cannot be made to distinguish, and should not be re-derived.** story-065 asked for one more thing than the design can give: that after a *squash*, the pre-story shape — the separate empty completion commit sitting on the escalation — report nothing, as it does after a rebase. It cannot, under any implementation of the reader. A squash writes one commit whose body is the folded commits' messages, and the pre-story shape's empty completion commit *has* a message, marker and all. Including the folded bodies is exactly what makes the marker travel, so the trunk carries identical evidence for both shapes; excluding them would satisfy this and lose the repaired and never-escalated shapes along with it. The assertion is left standing and failing in `tests/test_completion_survives_merge.py::test_the_pre_story_shape_is_lost_by_the_rebase_and_by_the_squash` rather than adjusted, with the reasoning at the assertion. The rebase half is the defect the story actually observed and is reproduced there. **The general shape: a criterion that asks a reader to distinguish two histories a merge method has already collapsed into one commit is asking for information that is no longer on the trunk.**

## The base a story branches from

Until story-030 neither entry point knew what a base branch was. `_checkout_story_branch` cut the story branch from whatever happened to be checked out, and `l5-plan` committed and pushed the plan artifact onto the same. story-029 was planned and run while `story/story-028` was checked out: `origin/story-028` came to hold the "Plan story-029" commit, and `story/story-029` was cut from thirty files of escalated, unaccepted work, costing a rebase and three hand-repointed assertions written against the wrong tree.

**Nothing has to be declared for the base to exist.** `resolve_base(target_root, config, base)` settles it in four steps, first answer winning: the `--base` argument, the target repository's optional `base_branch` config key, the branch `refs/remotes/origin/HEAD` names (read with `git symbolic-ref --quiet`, a non-zero return treated as unset rather than as a failure), and the literal `"main"`. The third step is the normal case and is why this needed no configuration to land in this repository; the fourth exists only so a repository publishing no `origin/HEAD` still has an answer, and it is exercised by a test rather than only documented. The derivation lives in exactly one function and both entry points read it, so `l5-run` and `l5-plan` cannot disagree about what a story branches from — the same one-fact-one-home rule as `story_branch` and `completion_commit_subject`.

**Two legs, checked in order, and only the first with something to say is printed.** `base_problems(target_root, base, declared)` returns the empty list for "go ahead". Leg one is that HEAD is standing on the base, because a branch is cut from what is checked out. Leg two is that the base matches its remote-tracking counterpart in *either* direction — behind, ahead or diverged all refuse, and identical proceeds — because a branch cut from a local base that is not the shared one is a branch nobody else can see the history of. When both hold, only the not-on-base message appears: the second is not actionable until the first is fixed, and two refusals for one act read as two problems. Which of the two was reported is what a test on this should assert, not merely that something was refused. The counterpart is found by `_base_tracking_ref` — the base's configured upstream first, because a branch stating one has stated the answer, then `refs/remotes/<remote>/<base>` for a branch pushed without tracking configuration.

**It refuses only on positive evidence**, the same one-directional bias `dirty_paths`, `unchanged_since_escalation` and `completion_commits` already take: a root that is not a git repository, a base with no remote-tracking counterpart, a repository with no remote and any failing git invocation each report *no* problem rather than a false one. A detached HEAD is the stated exception and is not an inconsistency — it is establishably not on the base, so it refuses like any other branch, with the message saying "a detached HEAD" rather than "branch HEAD". An *undeclared* base that does not resolve also says nothing: the harness guessed and established nothing. A *declared* one that does not resolve refuses, because that is the developer naming something absent.

**`--base` states a base; it does not skip the check.** There is deliberately no flag, environment variable or configuration key that bypasses the guard — the same reasoning as the clean-tree check, where a bypass on a correctness guard becomes the default invocation. What a declared base does is narrow the check to one question, that the ref resolves, because stating a base is stating a deliberate departure: branching one story from another story's branch is the case it exists for, so neither leg applies to it. A declared base is also the *start point* passed to `git checkout -b`; undeclared, the start point stays HEAD exactly as before, and the pre-flight is what makes that correct. That is why the undeclared checkout is byte-for-byte the behaviour it had.

**The check is creation-time, and that scope is the design rather than a simplification.** It answers "what will this branch be cut from", which is a question only a branch that does not yet exist has. An existing story branch is therefore never refused for its base, however far the base has moved — resume must keep working, and refusing a resume would refuse it for a decision a previous run already made. An existing branch that is behind gets a **note** instead: after `_checkout_story_branch`, when the branch existed before the run and `branch_behind` reports a positive count, one `kind="note"` event goes through the existing `append_event`, naming the base and the count, so `events.log` and `execution-history.json` stay two renderings of one write. No new schema and no new artifact — `execution-history.schema.json` already defines the `note` kind. Nothing routes on it, and rebasing the stale branch, automatically or on request, is out of scope: the note says the branch is behind and what to do about it stays the developer's call.

**The refusal happens above everything the run would create.** The guard sits between the finished-branch pre-flight and the clean-tree check, so a refused run leaves no run directory, no `state.json`, no log and no new branch, and invokes no agent — the shape every other pre-flight refusal takes. The target repository is only read: no commit, no branch, no checkout, no index change, no stash, and nothing reset or rebased on the developer's behalf.
