**`l5-plan` runs the same check for the same reason.** The script commits and pushes the artifact it caused to appear, and an artifact committed onto whatever happened to be checked out is exactly how story-029's plan landed on `origin/story-028`. `report()` resolves the base through `resolve_base` and calls `base_problems` after `plan_validation.artifact_problems` and *above* `plan_commit.commit_artifacts`, printing through the coordinator's own `_refuse_base` and returning 1 with nothing committed and nothing pushed; the artifacts stay where the session wrote them, which is the state to fix and re-run from. `--base <branch>` is parsed ahead of the request text and the remaining arguments go to the session as the request unchanged. `report`'s two new parameters default to `None`, so existing four-argument callers still work. This is the plan-time/pre-flight symmetry `refuse_bad_story` established: one function decides, one function prints, so the two entry points cannot report the same defect differently.

**One literal branch name now exists in `orchestration/`**, and `story_branch`'s docstring was amended rather than left standing beside code contradicting it. It used to promise "no branch name and no default base branch is written into orchestration"; `resolve_base`'s fallback breaks the second half, so the docstring now says what still holds, says the promise was revised, and says why — a fallback that is only documented is a fallback nobody has exercised. The literal is confined to that one line, and a search over `orchestration/` finding exactly one is the property to hold.

**This story's own run was not governed by the guard it adds**, for the fifth time in this pattern's history (`may_not_create`, `clean_clone`, `revert_check`, the pre-stage baseline, and now the base check): the coordinator process imports its module at start, so the run that landed this carried the pre-flight as it stood before. Enforcement begins with the next story. Expected, and stated in the story's constraints rather than discovered as a defect.

Worktrees and concurrent runs of two stories are out of scope, and the check assumes one checkout per target repository.

## Where a retry goes

Until story-028 every failed verification returned to the implementer, whatever had failed. The route was a constant — `on_failure.retry_stage`, read literally as `stage_names.index(stage["on_failure"]["retry_stage"])` — so the decision had no input at all. Neither verifier artifact named a stage: `verification-result.json` carried `status`, `retry_recommended` and `blocking_issues[].location`, `retry-guidance.json` carried `retry_scope`, and both of those are file paths with nothing mapping a path to a stage.

**story-011 is the observed cost, and it was paid twice.** Its verification failed on a defect wholly inside a test file the *tester* had created, and the verifier scoped the retry to exactly that one file. The coordinator routed to the implementer anyway: the implementer was given the wrong prompt for the job, and the stage that owned the artifact never got to repair its own output. story-011 is cited as evidence here and was neither re-run nor re-judged.

**The verifier names a category; the workflow says where a category goes.** The verifier's `on_failure` is now a `retry_routing` table, each entry carrying a destination `stage` and a `when` description of the defect it covers. The shipped table defines three since story-045: `implementation` → implementer, for a defect in the code under test; `validation` → tester, for a defect in the tests themselves — a wrong, missing or fragile assertion, one that cannot fail, or coverage that does not exercise what it claims; and `documentation` → documenter, for a defect in the documentation itself — a document describing behaviour the code does not have, naming a file, module or symbol that does not exist, contradicting what the run's own artifacts record, or omitting a change the story required be written down. The coordinator reads `verdict["retry_target"]` against that table and routes there. Adding a category is a workflow edit and nothing else: the categories are injected into the verifier's prompt from the same table the coordinator routes on, so the prompt restates nothing and a category cannot exist in one and not the other — and the third category is the first time that claim was tested rather than asserted.

**The `documentation` category's `when` carries a boundary the other two do not need**, and it is the line a verifier will actually stand on: judge the document against the code as it now stands, so a document that *accurately* describes wrong behaviour is an **implementation** defect and belongs to the category that owns the code. Without it the two categories overlap on every story where the documentation is the first place a reader meets the defect, and a route chosen by the wrong half sends the repair to a stage that cannot make it.

**No default route survives, and that is the whole point.** `retry_stage` was deleted rather than kept as a fallback. A recommended retry carrying no `retry_target`, and one naming a category the workflow does not define, each **escalate** — a silent fallback is precisely the drift the table exists to remove, and absorbing a malformed verdict into "send it to the implementer" would reproduce the old behaviour under a new name while looking like it had been fixed. Both escalations go through `_escalate`, so `retry_count` is untouched, and both sit **above** `archive_attempt`, so no `attempts/attempt-N/` directory is written: nothing is being superseded, and the artifacts at the run-directory root already describe the attempt that failed. Both reasons name the offending value *and* list the categories the workflow does define, in `events.log` and in `escalation-summary.md`, so the escalation is actionable without opening the workflow definition.

**Both sit above the ceiling comparison**, deliberately. When a verdict is unroutable *and* the ceiling has been reached, the reason a developer reads is the bug rather than the budget — the ceiling is a fact about how many attempts were spent, and a verdict that cannot be routed would have been a defect on the first attempt too. Since story-049 a third escalation sits between them and the comparison, on the same reasoning — see "A verdict that says retrying cannot finish" below — and since story-050 two further branches sit between *those two*: the guidance-mismatch escalation and the defective-guidance self-route, both below the unroutable pair so how an unroutable verdict is reported stays exactly what it was, and both above the unfinishable judgement so misdirection is ruled out before a confident exit. See "Guidance may not sanction the outcome it fails" below.

**The ceiling is single and global, and the reasoning should not be re-derived.** `max_retries: 2` lived in `rules/execution-rules.json` *and* in the workflow's `on_failure`, and only the rules copy was ever read; Chapter 13 tells the reader not to leave it in both, and two routes is the condition that passage leaves open. It is resolved against the per-route reading. `state.json` holds one `retry_count`; per-route ceilings need per-route counters, and an implementer/tester alternation would then run 2 + 2 attempts while neither route tripped its own ceiling early — and alternation is exactly the non-convergence a ceiling exists to catch. The division is clean: the ceiling protects the run's budget, which is a property of the run; routing decides where an attempt goes, the ceiling decides how many attempts exist at all. `escalation_rules.max_retries_exceeded` is unaffected. Since story-062 the counter it is compared against is scoped to the run's current *entry*: a resume restores the allowance rather than resuming with it already spent, so the ceiling bounds one entry's attempts and re-entering is the deliberate act that grants more. `max_retries` itself is unchanged, and so is every condition that escalates — see "Resuming a run" above.

**The table is validated when the workflow loads, not when a retry happens.** `retry_routing_problems(stages)` reports two problems and they are the only two a definition can state without knowing what any run will do:

| Declared route | Outcome |
| --- | --- |
| Destination is not a stage the workflow defines | Refused, naming the category and the stage |
| Destination is at or after the stage declaring it | Refused, saying that routing forward would skip verification |

A table naming a stage that does not exist is a defect in the definition, and a run that discovers it three stages in has already spent an implementer, a tester and a verifier on it. The refusal takes the established pre-flight shape through the shared `refuse`: exit 1, one message per problem, no run directory, no `state.json`, no log, no branch created or checked out, and no agent invoked. The clean-clone route is held to both checks alongside the categories, because it is a route the workflow declares even though nothing chooses it.

**Nothing in orchestration knows a category name or a destination.** Both come off the loaded workflow, the way `stage_restrictions` and `may_not_create` already do, and a search over `orchestration/story_coordinator.py` and `orchestration/context_assembler.py` finding neither is the property to hold. The route taken is recorded rather than recomputed: the execution-history entry for a routed retry carries `retry_category` and `retry_stage` beside `retry_decision` and `retry_reason` through the same `append_event` call, so `events.log` and `execution-history.json` stay two renderings of one write, and `retry-history.json` records the destination the run actually routed to rather than a constant — no change to its schema, which already had somewhere to put it.

**A stage receiving a retry now knows it is on one, and why.** The injected `retry_state` carries the category and the destination alongside `retry_iteration` and the ceiling, and `prompts/tester.md` gained the retry placeholders it never needed while the tester could only be reached going forward.

**What story-028 deliberately left out landed as story-036.** The generalized mechanism the originating request also describes — mechanical failures self-routing in place, `self_route_count` against a per-stage `max_self_routes`, the `prompt-<stage>-attempt-N-try-M.md` naming addition, and coordinator-authored evidence for a self-routed stage — was the follow-up half, scoped with the developer on 2026-08-10 and settled on 2026-08-08 in the book. Nothing in story-028 was a partial build of it. The instruction it carried forward held: **the two budgets are not conflated.** What `retry_count` bounds is backward routes only, which is what it has always meant; the self-route budget bounds re-entries in place and reads `retry_count` and `max_retries` not at all. The provisional stale-required-output escalation is the destination that mechanism changed, as predicted.

**This harness now diverges from Appendix A's excerpts.** The appendix builds this system from an empty directory and prints `workflows/story-workflow.json` and the coordinator's verification branch as they stood at the `appendix-a` tag: a workflow whose `on_failure` is `{"retry_stage": "implementer", "max_retries": 2}`, a `clean_clone` declared as a bare artifact name, and a coordinator that indexes `stage_names` on that constant. All three are gone. A reader following the appendix and then reading this repository will find the routing table, the widened `clean_clone` object, the single ceiling in `rules/execution-rules.json`, and the two escalations in place of them; the appendix's build is still a correct level 3 harness, and this is the divergence rather than an erratum. The book documents the design this story implements in Chapters 14 and 17 and states it as a contract in `outputs/artifact-naming-changes-for-harness-repo.md`. **That manuscript is not in this checkout**, so the story was planned against the request's summary of the contract, and the conformance check against Chapter 17's retry-management section is an outstanding item carried forward rather than one performed here.

**story-045 widens that divergence rather than opening a new one.** The appendix's excerpts describe the four stages in the previous order — implementer, tester, verifier, documenter, with the documenter printed last and the coordinator's verification branch printed against that arrangement — and the shipped workflow now runs the documenter third. A reader following the appendix and then reading this repository will find the stage list reordered and a third routing category beside the two the appendix has none of; the appendix's build remains a correct level 3 harness, and this is the same kind of divergence as the routing table itself, not an erratum in either.

## Guidance may not sanction the outcome it fails

The verifier writes `retry-guidance.json` to direct a retry, and until story-050 nothing stood between two checks. The coordinator checked the guidance's *shape* against `schemas/retry-guidance.schema.json` — three arrays of free strings — and checked the verdict's *route* strictly, with no default and no fallback. Between them, guidance could instruct a stage to deliver a partial result and the same verifier could then fail the run for delivering it.

**story-048's tester is on record as complying and being failed for it.** The guidance rendered into `prompt-tester-attempt-3.md` asked for the whole job in its first instruction — empty the awaiting-conversion list by converting the seventeen modules it names — and *authorized not doing it* in its third: for any module that cannot be converted within this retry, rewrite its reason so it discloses that its workflow read is an input awaiting conversion. The fourth commissioned the machinery that tracks exactly what does not get converted. The tester delivered all four, converting two modules and rewriting eight disclosures, and the final verdict praised the machinery as "a working control" while failing the run for not emptying the list. **The rate everyone read as a stage underperforming was the guidance working as written.**

**The signature is behavioural and needs no reading of prose**: the retry delivered what its guidance asked, and verification failed anyway. That contradiction indicts the guidance rather than the stage, and making it computable takes two additions and no natural-language analysis.

**Guidance declares its own success condition, at the moment it is written.** Each `current_focus` entry now carries `satisfied_when` beside `focus`: the observable condition that would satisfy it, written before the outcome is known. That is what makes a sanctioning clause *visible* rather than merely present — story-048's third instruction would have had to write "the disclosure reasons are rewritten" as the success condition of an entry whose real criterion was that the list is empty. A condition arrived at afterwards can be fitted to the outcome, and then guidance that authorized a partial result and a verdict that failed it are indistinguishable. That reasoning is recorded in the property's own description, not only here.

**The next verdict answers it entry by entry.** `verification-result.json`'s `guidance_outcomes` carries one object per entry of the guidance in force, echoing that entry verbatim in `guidance`, with an optional `unmet` whose presence is the signal that the retry did not meet it and whose content is why — the shape story-049 chose for `unfinishable_by_retry`, for the reason `retry_target` already records: no `if`/`then`, no `dependentRequired`, so a flag with a sibling reason has a state where the flag is set and the reason is missing, and presence-as-signal has none. **Every `current_focus` focus and every `preserve_behavior` string must be accounted for**, so a retry that regressed behaviour it was told to preserve fails normally instead of reading as defective guidance.

**The coordinator compares two sets of strings and reads none of them as language.** `guidance_entries` flattens a guidance to the strings a verdict must account for — each entry's `focus` plus every `preserve_behavior` string, and *not* `satisfied_when`, because the verdict answers the entry rather than restating its condition. `compare_guidance_outcomes` returns the two directions of set difference and the set of entries carrying `unmet`; a misquoted entry appears as one `missing` and one `extra`. No branch anywhere inspects what a guidance entry, an unmet reason or a blocking issue *says*, which is the property to hold: varying the wording of either while holding the sets equal must not change the routing.

On a failed verdict, when guidance was in force:

- **The echoed set is not exactly the guidance in force** — missing, extra, misquoted or absent altogether — and the run **escalates naming the mismatch**, with no default and no assumption about what silence meant, the same strictness `retry_target` is already held to. It goes through `_escalate`, so `retry_count` is untouched and no `attempts/attempt-N/` is written. `guidance_mismatch_reason` names the unaccounted entries and the entries the guidance does not carry *separately*, and says outright when the verdict carried no `guidance_outcomes` at all — a developer should be able to tell an absent one from a wrong one from the recorded reason alone, and both from a met set and an unmet one.
- **Every entry was met and the verdict still failed**, so the guidance is defective. **A defective-guidance finding spends no retry budget.** The verifier runs again in place on the self-route budget its own stage already declares, told by the coordinator what the contradiction is, so it can rewrite guidance that does not authorize the outcome it will fail, or fail the work on the criterion it actually failed.

**The fourth self-route failure belongs beside the three mechanical ones for the same reason they do**: it is a fact *computed from* what the stage produced, not a judgement about the work. `DEFECTIVE_RETRY_GUIDANCE` routes through the **existing** `self_route` call rather than a new path, so it inherits every property a self-route already has — neither `retry_count` nor `max_retries` spent or read, nothing archived under `attempts/attempt-N/`, nothing appended to `retry-history.json`, and the attempt number unmoved in every rendered prompt filename — and, when the verifier's budget is already spent, `self_route` returns its existing exhausted-budget reason and the run escalates naming both halves. **Escalation is the fallback without a second escalation path being written for it.** Because two verdicts are written inside one attempt, the run directory holds a second `verification/iteration-N.json` for it; that is what a self-routed verifier looks like on disk.

`self_route_statement` gained a fourth branch and an optional `entries` argument, threaded through `self_route`. It names the guidance entries that were met and the verdict that failed anyway, written out verbatim rather than counted, in the coordinator's own voice as the other three statements are — a re-running verifier told only that it contradicted itself has nothing to act on.

**The check sits ahead of `unfinishable_by_retry` deliberately.** story-049 gave the verifier an honest exit; a fast exit paired with guidance that still sanctions partial results is *worse* than what came before it, because the verifier then escalates confidently on runs that were only stalling because it misdirected them. Misdirection is ruled out first, so a verdict carrying both a fully-met `guidance_outcomes` and `unfinishable_by_retry` self-routes rather than ending the run.

**The guidance in force is state, not history.** `RunState.guidance_in_force` is set at the two places a retry is routed, from the *same freshness-checked view* the `retry-history.json` entry is built from — `append_retry_record`'s return value — so a stale guidance left at the run root can never be read as in force, and nothing reads `retry-history.json` or the `attempts/` archive to route. That is the repository's standing rule, and `retry-history.schema.json` says outright that history is evidence: every routing decision must be reconstructable from `state.json` and `events.log` alone. Setting it where a retry carries freshly written guidance and clearing it where a retry carries none is also what keeps the **clean-clone reroute** — which follows a *passing* verdict and writes no guidance — outside the check for free, rather than by a second rule naming that path. The field defaults to empty, so an existing `state.json` still loads and empty means a verification routes exactly as a first verification does.

**No configured scalar, threshold or budget is introduced**, the workflow definition is unchanged — no new stage, no new retry category, no new declared output, no new budget key — and the shape change reaches a retried stage with no edit to `orchestration/context_assembler.py` and none to the retried stages' prompts, because guidance is injected as the file's own text.

**Nothing here judges guidance quality.** The claim is narrow and mechanical: guidance may not authorize the outcome it will fail. A verifier could still evade the check by reporting an entry unmet that was met, and nothing can stop that; it is the same trust boundary `retry_target` already sits on, and the answer is the same — the record says what was claimed.

**The criterion is stated in `prompts/verifier.md`**, in three paragraphs beside the retry-guidance schema injection: what `satisfied_when` must carry and why it is written now, that every entry of the guidance in force must be accounted for and echoed verbatim and judged against the condition written when the entry was written, and what a defective-guidance self-route means and what to do on arriving at one. The template states no part of the shape — type, optionality and required-ness come from the injected schemas.

`tests/test_defective_retry_guidance.py` is the standing regression module, driving a real run for each behaviour rather than calling the routing branch directly, with a control for every absence it asserts. The distinction the story lives or dies on is the one to keep pointed at: **a retry that reports one entry unmet is ordinary under-delivery and routes exactly as a failed verdict always has.** That is also why the existing modules' fixtures now route their failed verdicts through `conftest.answering_guidance`, which reads `state.json`'s `guidance_in_force` and reports every entry unmet — every subject in those modules is unchanged, and the fixture answers exactly what the coordinator will compare it against.

## A verdict that says retrying cannot finish

`verification-result.json` carries one optional string since story-049, `unfinishable_by_retry`: the verifier's judgement that no number of retries closes the gap between what remains and what an attempt delivers, together with the split it recommends between this story and a follow-on. On a failed verdict the coordinator escalates on the **first sighting** of it, and the escalation's recorded reason is the verifier's own text.

**story-048 is the observed cost.** It escalated after three full retry cycles and roughly two hours of wall clock, having converted 5 modules and left 14 awaiting conversion — 0 in the first attempt, then 3, then 2. (The awaiting set also shrinks by reclassification, so 22 reported minus 14 awaiting is the subject-reader count, not a conversion count.) Nothing refused it and nothing stopped it early: no timeout, no cost ceiling, no mechanical failure, `self_route_count` 0, every stage logging `stage-completed`. The escalation was reached by *spending the entire retry budget* rather than by anyone judging the budget could not cover the work. The verifier had already reached that judgement unaided and written it into `retry-guidance.json` — "the per-attempt rate is what is failing, not the approach" — and had nowhere to act on it. It could recommend another retry of a rate it had just called failing, or decline a retry with no way to distinguish a judgement from a giving-up. This field is that way to say so; with it, story-048 would have ended after the first verification rather than the third.

**Plan-time size was the obvious check and it does not separate the failure.** The originating request asked for one: refuse a story whose assigned work is too large for a stage to finish inside the retry budget. Across the committed corpus the largest per-stage `likely_file_changes` counts are story-038 with 35 assigned to one stage and story-044 with 21, both of which completed in a single attempt, against story-048's 32, which did not. The quantifier shape does not separate them either — story-038's task reads "Rename the thirty-four modules" and story-044's "Update the fake runners in every tests/ module", and both finished. What differed was the cost of each item: a uniform rename against a per-module classification and rewrite. No plan-time check reads that, and any ceiling low enough to refuse story-048 refuses two stories that landed first time. **The axis that predicts the failure is progress against remaining work, and it is only visible once a run is underway** — which is why this is a verdict and not a validator. `orchestration/plan_validation.py` keeps its five classes of problem exactly as they were.

**Since story-050 two branches sit above it**, and the reason is in the section before this one: a fast exit paired with guidance that still sanctions partial results lets the verifier escalate confidently on runs it was itself misdirecting, so a verdict carrying both a fully-met `guidance_outcomes` and `unfinishable_by_retry` self-routes the verifier rather than ending the run on the unfinishable judgement. A verdict with no guidance in force, or one reporting an entry unmet, reaches this branch exactly as it did before.

**It is read above the ceiling comparison**, for the same reason the two unroutable-retry escalations are: a verdict saying retrying cannot finish must never be weighed against how much budget is left, or the run spends a budget the verifier has just said cannot cover the work. The branch sits immediately after those two and before the comparison of `state.retry_count` against `rules["max_retries"]`, and it reuses the same `_escalate` call rather than adding a second escalation path. It therefore inherits that path's properties rather than restating them: `retry_count` is not incremented, `archive_attempt` is not reached so no `attempts/attempt-N/` directory is written, and the artifacts at the run-directory root are the ones describing the attempt that just ended. The escalation is recorded with `retry_decision: escalate` and the verifier's text as `retry_reason`, so `escalation-summary.md`'s `## Reason` section and the `execution-history` entry are written by the code that already writes them and carry the judgement rather than a coordinator paraphrase of it. **The budget is genuinely unspent when this happens**, and that is the property to assert: the recorded retry count is below the ceiling at the moment the escalation is recorded, so the escalation is a judgement rather than a ceiling reached early.

**Presence is the signal and content is the reason**, one string rather than a boolean with a sibling reason. `retry_target` beside it already records why a conditional requirement cannot be expressed here — the validator subset has no `if`/`then` and no `dependentRequired`, so "reason required when the flag is true" is inexpressible. Presence-as-signal leaves no state where the signal is set and the reason is missing. Nothing reads a separate flag and nothing infers the judgement from the absence of a retry recommendation.

**It is read only where it can mean something.** The branch sits in the `elif` chain below `status == "passed"`, so a passing verdict carrying the field never reaches it and completes exactly as a passing verdict without it does, clean-clone check included — a passing verification routes nowhere, so nothing there could act on the judgement. A failed verdict *without* the field reaches none of the new code, and every existing routing behaviour is unchanged: a recommended retry below the ceiling still reroutes, an unroutable target still escalates naming the defined categories, an exhausted budget still escalates naming the ceiling.

**A verdict carrying both the field and `retry_recommended: true` contradicts itself, and neither half is silently preferred.** It escalates with a composed reason naming the contradiction and still carrying the verifier's text, so the record is distinguishable from both an ordinary declined retry and an ordinary routed one. One corner follows from the branch ordering and is recorded rather than papered over: a verdict carrying the field, `retry_recommended: true` *and* no (or an unknown) `retry_target` is caught by the existing unroutable-retry branch first and escalates naming the missing or unknown category rather than the contradiction. That preserves the existing routing for those two branches exactly, which the story required, and the doubly-malformed verdict still escalates with the retry count untouched. Moving the new branch above them would have changed how an unroutable verdict is reported.

**The criterion is stated in `prompts/verifier.md`, beside the `retry_target` paragraph** — where a verifier reading about routing meets the decision not to route. It asks that the judgement be made from what this attempt actually delivered against what the story still asks, said on the first verification that sees it rather than as a retry that repeats the rate, and that the split be named: what remains, what this attempt delivered, and which parts a first story should carry against a follow-on. A judgement without that split says only that the work is too big. The prompt names the field and states no part of its shape — type, optionality and required-ness come from `{{verification_result_schema}}`, already injected into that template.

**No configured scalar, threshold or budget is introduced anywhere**, and that matters beyond simplicity: `rules/` is a blocked path for every stage of every story, so a scalar declared there could not have been written by the run that needed it. This design needs no scalar anywhere — the verdict is a judgement and the coordinator obeys it without weighing it against a number. The workflow definition is unchanged: no new stage, no new retry category, no new declared output, no new `RunState` field and no new artifact.

`tests/test_unfinishable_retry_verdict.py` is the standing regression module. It drives a real run for each behaviour rather than calling the routing branch directly, against a workflow the test builds for itself, and every absence it asserts — no further stage invoked, no attempt archive written, no retry consumed — carries a control demonstrating the same assertion fails when the behaviour is violated. That control is the part most easily lost: an assertion that no further stage ran passes equally when nothing ran and when the assertion stopped looking.

## The documenter runs before the verifier

Until story-045 the order was implementer → tester → verifier → documenter, and the documenter's position bought two gaps that were the same gap seen from two sides.

**Nothing judged the documentation.** The verifier ran before the stage that wrote it, so seven consecutive runs — story-034, 037, 039, 040, 041, 042 and 043 — carry a verifier note saying the documenter's output could not be judged. A stage whose work no stage evaluates is a stage whose acceptance criteria are unfalsifiable.

**And the clean-clone check ran on a tree the documenter had not finished writing.** The check is declared on the verifier and runs on its passing verdict, so under the old order the documenter edited the tree *after* the check cloned it and ran the suite in it. story-043 is where that cost something and it is worth stating concretely: its documenter wrote a sentence into this document naming a `tests/` module the same story had deleted, story-038's `test_every_tests_path_the_architecture_document_names_exists` failed on it, all three CI jobs went red, and the run's own execution history shows `clean-clone-passed` minutes *before* the stage that broke it. A completed run could ship a red suite, and the check that exists to prevent exactly that had already reported.

One reorder closes both. The verifier is now last, so it judges the documenter's output through `{{documentation_report}}` and `{{documenter_changed_files}}`, and the clean-clone check clones a tree that already holds the documenter's edits. On a passing run the execution history now shows `clean-clone-passed` after the documenter's `stage-completed` entry and before `story-completed`, which is the ordering story-043 needed and did not have.

**The reorder and the third route are inseparable, in both directions**, which is why they landed as one story rather than one and a follow-on. Without the route, story-028's rule that a missing or unrecognised category escalates rather than falling back means every documentation finding the verifier could now make becomes an escalation, because no category owns it. And the route cannot land first: `retry_routing_problems` refuses a destination that does not sit strictly before the declaring stage, so `documentation` → documenter with the documenter *after* the verifier makes every run refuse at pre-flight. Either half alone is a broken definition.

**The cost is recorded as a decision rather than discovered in a bill: documentation is redone on every retry.** A failing verdict re-enters at the implementer or the tester, and the documenter now sits on the way back, so each retry costs one extra documenter invocation — roughly two to four dollars on this repository's runs. That cost is precisely why the stage was placed last originally. It was accepted because the alternative is documentation nothing judges, and because it is paid only when a story retries; a run that passes first time invokes the documenter exactly once, which is asserted rather than assumed. A redone documentation stage spends no retry: the ceiling in `rules/execution-rules.json` and the way retries are counted are untouched.

**A fifth stage was considered and rejected**: a documentation verifier with its own prompt, routing and retry budget. The reorder gets the same judgement from the verifier that already exists and gets the clean-clone guarantee for free, where a fifth stage gets the judgement, costs a stage, and leaves the clean-clone ordering exactly where it was. Settled with the developer on 2026-08-15.

**One thing the documenter is told changed as a side effect, and is left as it is.** Its `{{verification_result}}` renders as `None` on a first attempt, because it no longer runs after a verdict; on a retry it still receives the previous attempt's verdict from the run-directory root. Nothing routes on it and no behaviour depends on it. Changing what the documenter is told was outside story-045; a story that wants the documenter to see a verdict on the first pass has to decide what verdict that would be.

**story-045 could not verify itself**, for the reason story-028 and story-030 already lived through: the coordinator loads the workflow at run start, so the run that lands the reorder executes under the old order, with its own documenter running last and unjudged. This section was written by that unjudged documenter. The first run *governed* by the new order is the next story.

## The documenter may not assert what nothing can check

This document is tracked and permanent. The evidence the documenter writes it from is not: `.gitignore` excludes `.harness/runs/`, `.harness/logs/` and `.harness/requests/`, and `git ls-files .harness/runs/` returns nothing. So a documenter could write a factual claim into the repository whose only support is a file local to one machine, invisible to the verifier that judges it, and absent from any clone. The reorder above closed the gap that nothing judged the documentation; it did not close this one, because the verifier's evidence horizon is its own branch and its own run directory, and the documenter was free to write past it.

**The observed case is in this document's own history.** `4ec1504`, "Correct story-048's conversion count in ARCHITECTURE.md", repairs a sentence story-049's documenter wrote about story-048; the sentence as it stood before that commit, and the corrected text beside it, are both readable from git. The verifier of the run that wrote it recorded the inconsistency and said it could not be settled from the artifacts available, which was exactly true — story-049 branched from story-048's plan commit, so the ground truth was on a branch it had no reason to visit. **Detection was luck**: the figure was visible only because it contradicted a breakdown printed in the same sentence. A wrong number with no adjacent arithmetic passes silently and stays wrong forever, so this class of defect is undetectable by inspection, counting occurrences is not available as evidence, and story-051 was filed on one sighting.

**The seam is the reference, not the sentence.** Nothing here reads prose to decide what a factual claim is: that is not tractable, and a check that half-works is worse than none — the lesson story-050 already records. A claim about another story names it in a fixed shape, `story-0NN`, and whether that story's work is in this repository's history is a question git answers. So the whole decision is mechanical: text this run added to a configured architecture document, naming a story with no completion commit reachable from the run's base, in a block that also carries a quantity, is reported. `claim_support_check` in `orchestration/story_coordinator.py` composes four small functions to say that — `added_blocks`, `story_references`, `carries_a_quantity` and `unsupported_claims` — and takes the merged decision from `completion_commits(target_root, base, story)`, the same reader the finished-branch pre-flight uses, so "has this story's work landed" is one fact rather than a second spelling of it.

**The forward-reference case survives structurally rather than by a judgement**, and that is the distinction this lives or dies on. This document legitimately says "the next story in this line" and describes the story currently landing, which is by definition unmerged when the documenter writes it. A phrase-shaped forward reference carries no story number at all — a story number does not exist until the story is planned — so it is invisible to a check keyed on the reference; and the run's own story id is subtracted from every block's references outright. Neither exemption is a rule about intent, and neither reads what the text says.

**The remedy is attribution, not refusal.** Describing a motivating failure is legitimate and useful, so refusing the claim outright would be too strong. "The story-048 request records 14 remaining" is checkable as a quotation and honest about its provenance, where "converted 8 of 22" asserts a fact the repository cannot hold. `prompts/documenter.md` therefore states that the untracked directories are not citable authority and gives the two ways to keep a fact worth keeping: restate it in terms the repository can hold, or attribute it as a quotation naming its source. That prompt rule is the preventive half, and the check is what makes it enforceable.

**The check reports and routes nothing.** It appends no retry-history entry, consumes no retry and no self-route, and adds no escalation path: a run whose record reports a claim is routed exactly as the same run with an empty record. The record is injected into the verifier as `claim_support_result`, beside `clean_clone_result`, and `prompts/verifier.md` says what a report means (a claim made with no support that travels — *not* a claim that a number is wrong), what settles it, and that a claim left asserted is a documentation defect belonging to the retry category the injected routing table gives to one. The coordinator computes the fact; the stage whose job is judging decides what to do with it, which is the same division `clean_clone_result` already takes.

**What the check does not say is stated in the artifact itself.** `schemas/claim-support-result.schema.json`'s top-level description carries it, because the artifact is where a reader meets the verdict and where a false reading of it would be formed: the check never asks whether a reported figure is right, and an empty `reports` array is not a statement that the document's figures are true. `ran` and `reason` follow the optional-by-absence convention `clean-clone-result` and `revert-check-result` use — a target configuring no architecture documents, a base that does not resolve and a failing `git diff` each record `ran: false` with the reason, so none of them reads as a document with no unsupportable claims.

**Two scope limits are stated rather than papered over.** A quantity is a digit, or one word of a bounded set — the cardinals two through nineteen, the tens twenty through ninety, hundred, thousand, million and dozen, and the ordinals second through nineteenth plus the -ieth forms — matched case-insensitively and only as a whole word, so "eight of twenty-two" is seen and "often" is not "ten". `one`, `a`, `an` and `first` are excluded on purpose rather than overlooked: they saturate ordinary prose and enumerate nothing, so admitting them would report almost every added block naming an unmerged story. Beyond the set is beyond the check — "quadrillion", and any figure that would need the text read as language, are quantities this does not see. The set is written once in `orchestration/story_coordinator.py` as `NUMBER_WORDS`, with the exclusion reasoned beside it so a reader can tell an omission from a decision. **Widening was cheap because the check reports and never routes**, which is the general point: a false report costs the verifier a moment's judgement rather than a retry, so the conservatism a digit-only test defends buys very little — and it demonstrably missed the case story-054 was filed on, story-047's own wording, "a fourth standing rule" against a module carrying more, spelled out and therefore invisible. And a block is a paragraph, not a hunk: a reference and a quantity in one paragraph are one claim, while the same two a page apart are not. Re-verifying figures already in the document, tracking `.harness/runs/`, and giving the verifier cross-branch access all stay out of scope — the run directory, the logs and the requests stay untracked, and making the claim checkable did not depend on tracking them.

**The stage the check runs after is workflow-owned.** `workflows/story-workflow.json` declares `"claim_support": {"result": "claim-support-result.json"}` on the documenter stage, shaped like the `clean_clone` and `revert_check` declarations beside it: one key names the artifact and turns the check on. The call site reads `stage.get("claim_support")` in the stage loop and names no stage, so a workflow that moves the declaration runs the check after that stage instead, and one that omits it writes no record — with no change to orchestration code. The documents scanned come from the target's own `architecture_docs` configuration, and no document path appears in harness source. Every target therefore inherits both the defect and the fix.

**It has its own event kind, `claim-support-checked`, and the reason is a control rather than taste.** The first implementation used the existing `note` kind and `tests/test_branch_base.py::test_a_branch_that_is_not_behind_gets_no_note` went red: that control reads the history for entries of kind `note` and asserts there are none, so sharing the kind would have made the stale-base note's own control report this check instead, and the assertion would have had to be narrowed by message to survive. `schemas/execution-history.schema.json` gained the one enum value, which is what keeps a run's history valid against the schema it is validated with.

**story-051's own run was not governed by the check it adds**, for the stale-workflow reason story-007, story-028, story-030 and story-045 already record: the coordinator loads the workflow at run start, so the declaration this run's implementer added to the shipped workflow is not in the definition this run is executing, and this section was written with the check not watching. Enforcement begins with the next story.

## A stage that failed mechanically runs again

Three failures used to end a run that a second attempt would plausibly fix: the agent process died, a required output was never written, or the required output at the run root is a previous attempt's. None is a judgement about the work — the stage simply did not produce what it declared, and no verifier ever saw it. story-028's implementer is the observed case: it died on a dropped connection 127 turns and 34 minutes in, and the coordinator escalated with `retry_count` untouched, leaving $14.12 of substantial work needing a manual resume.

**A fourth failure joined the three since story-050**, and it is not mechanical: retry guidance that was met in full by a retry the same verifier's verdict then failed. It belongs here for the reason the three do rather than because it looks like them — it is a fact computed from what the stage produced, not a judgement about the work — and it reuses this whole mechanism unchanged, budget included. See "Guidance may not sanction the outcome it fails" above; everything below holds for it verbatim.

**Mechanical failures route differently from verdicts.** There is no defect to categorize and no other stage to send the work to, so the failed stage runs again: a **self-route**. That is exactly the reasoning that does *not* apply to a boundary violation, where retrying the same instructions would produce the same violation again — so schema violations, blocked paths, stage output ownership, the revert check's two escalations and the clean-clone check's two are all unchanged, for a stage that declares a budget as much as for one that does not.

**Landing it changed nothing until a workflow opted in.** The budget is `max_self_routes` on a stage, and a stage that declares none escalates on all three failures exactly as it did before, with `retry_count` untouched and no run-directory artifact named for a self-route. The shipped workflow declared `1` on the implementer and nothing anywhere else when the mechanism landed; it now declares `1` on the implementer, the verifier and the documenter, and `2` on the tester, so no stage of it is budget-less. That compatibility property is still shown against a stage that declares none rather than argued, but the stage is one `tests/test_self_routing_retry.py` builds rather than one the deployment keeps back — see the budget bullets at the end of this file for how that changed.

**A budget above one has a middle, and until story-047 nothing walked it.** While every budget was `1`, "spend the budget" and "self-route once" were the same event, so a coordinator that stopped after the first self-route would have passed every case in `tests/test_self_routing_retry.py`. A budget of `2` separates them: an invocation that has already self-routed once fails again and is *still* re-run in place, and only the failure after that escalates. The cases that walk it are parametrized over **every declaration with room for more than one** — read off the workflow, never written down — with a companion assertion that some stage declares such a budget, so a parametrization that collected nothing reports as an absence of tests rather than an absence of failures.

**The global ceiling survives the exception, because a self-route cannot alternate.** When it succeeds the workflow advances, and the only way it repeats is by failing again in the same place — which is why one counter can bound it and why the implementer/tester alternation that ruled out per-route ceilings has no analogue here. A self-route therefore **spends neither `retry_count` nor `max_retries` and reads neither**: no `attempts/attempt-N/` directory is written, no entry is appended to `retry-history.json`, and the attempt number does not move in any rendered prompt filename. `rules/execution-rules.json` still holds exactly one definition of `max_retries`, and nothing in the self-route path touches it.

**One decision function, and since story-050 four call sites.** `self_route(run_dir, state, stage, *, failure, reason, artifacts, attempt, entries=None) -> SelfRouteDecision` is where a self-routing failure is decided; the sites route through it and re-enter the loop at the same index when it reports `taken`. Each site keeps the reason text it already escalated with, and that same string is what the evidence artifact records and what the escalation names, so a self-route and an escalation of the same failure describe it identically. When a declared budget is exhausted the reason names both halves — the mechanical failure *and* that the stage has exhausted its self-route budget of N — so a developer learns what failed and why the stage stopped trying. The budget comes off the loaded stage dict; no stage name, prefix, artifact name or budget value is written into orchestration code.

**The count is live state, not history.** `RunState.self_route_count` defaults to `0`, so an existing `state.json` still loads and a missing value means no self-route has occurred. It is scoped implicitly by `current_stage` and holds only the live count: it is zeroed at every entry to a stage that is not itself a self-route — advancing, a retry's reroute, and a resume all reset it — so a stage that self-routes, succeeds, and whose run later fails mechanically at another stage gives that stage its full budget. Past self-routes are read from the history instead: one `self-routed` entry per self-route, naming the stage as both origin and destination, so counting them per stage is a query rather than a second number kept somewhere. `save_state` runs inside `self_route`, so a crash mid-stage leaves a count a reader can trust.

**A self-routed stage has no agent-authored guidance, so the coordinator states why itself.** `self_route_statement` composes it, and the statement says outright that the coordinator wrote it and that no verifier saw the work. A missing or stale output is *named*; a failed agent process is not, and the difference is deliberate — the invocation did not reach the point of declaring what it had done, so the statement says it exited without completing and that the working tree already holds whatever it wrote before it did. A routable failure without injected evidence replays the conditions that produced it, which is why this is injected rather than left to the stage to infer.

**The evidence is an artifact on disk, not only a prompt string**, so a run directory can say why a stage ran twice. `self-route-<stage>-attempt-N-try-M.json` is keyed by stage, attempt and try together — a stage can self-route more than once within an attempt and more than one stage can self-route in a run — so no self-route overwrites another's. Optional fields are absent rather than null, as `clean-clone-result` and `execution-history` already are: a failed agent process carries no `artifacts`. The prompt is read back off that artifact rather than passed along in memory, so what the prompt says and what the run directory records cannot differ.

**Two names have one home each.** `self_route_result_file` and `prompt_file` both shape their name in one function and both accept `"*"` for the try number, so the glob that *finds* what was written and the call that *writes* it share one spelling. `prompt_file` is what makes a re-run write `prompt-<stage>-attempt-N-try-M.md` while a first invocation writes exactly the `prompt-<stage>-attempt-N.md` it has always written — the suffix appears only when a self-route occurred, and a run with no self-route writes precisely the prompt filenames it wrote before. Without it, a re-run under an unmoved attempt number would write over the prompt the failed invocation was actually given, which is the evidence the re-run exists to build on. That in turn is why `interrupted_attempt_artifacts` gained an optional `run_dir`: a resume zeroes the count, so a resumed stage's first mechanical failure is try 1 of the same attempt again and lands on names the interrupted attempt already wrote. Those names cannot be derived from the workflow — the count that produced them is live state the resume has discarded — so they are read off the directory by `self_route_artifacts` when a run directory is given, and a caller that asks only what the workflow declares gets exactly what it got before.

**A malformed budget is refused at pre-flight**, through the existing path: exit 1, one message per problem, no run directory, no `state.json`, no log, no branch created and no agent invoked. Same reasoning as the routing table — a budget that is not a count cannot be spent, and every run under that definition would meet it.

**What a self-route must not be decided against is its own leftovers.** See the stage-baseline discussion under "Run directory anatomy" and the story-036 bullets below: the merge story-037 landed admits a governed path only when another stage's changed-files record accounts for it, so a crashed invocation's partial work stays out of the baseline while the tester's file across a backward retry still comes in.

## A run has a cost ceiling

Until story-063 nothing bounded what a run could spend. `max_retries` bounds how many attempts exist and `max_self_routes` bounds how many times a stage runs again in place, but neither is a statement about money: a single stage invocation has cost more than a whole story while both counters stood still. The number was already arriving — every stage invocation ends with a result event carrying `total_cost_usd`, which `run_agent` parsed and discarded. The change is to **carry** it rather than to compute it, and never to re-derive it by reading the log back, which would be a second parser of the harness's own output.

**Two ceilings, and they bound different things.** The per-execution ceiling, `max_execution_cost_usd` on a stage, bounds **one call**: every invocation of that stage is handed exactly that allowance, passed down to the agent CLI as `--max-budget-usd`, so the invocation stops itself rather than being stopped afterwards. The run ceiling, `max_run_cost_usd` on the workflow, bounds **repetition**: the current entry's total spend is compared against it immediately above the call that invokes the stage agent. Both are optional, and **absent means unbounded** — a definition declaring neither produces a run indistinguishable from a pre-story-063 one, down to the arguments the runner is called with and the command `run_agent` builds from them.

**A ceiling checked only between stages would be a gate and nothing more**, because the invocation that runs after the gate opens would be unbounded. The sharpest evidence is a single implementer invocation that spent $14.12 before its connection dropped: no between-stage check would have seen it, because the stage never returned. The per-execution allowance is what makes a ceiling a ceiling — it is enforced *inside* the invocation as well as before it, and the two halves are why both exist.

**The per-execution allowance is flat.** A stage's declared ceiling is handed to every invocation of it unmodified — not reduced by what that stage has already spent, and not reduced by what the run has left. No cumulative per-stage total is kept, compared or subtracted anywhere. A cumulative stage ceiling starves a legitimate retry: this harness's self-routes are stages that did their work and failed to write their artifacts, and the re-run has to redo that work, so handing it a remainder invites it to fail again for a new reason. Repetition stays bounded by `max_self_routes`, `max_retries` and the run ceiling, which cap how many times a stage runs at all. A third, cumulative per-stage ceiling was considered and **rejected** as redundant against those, and because stage dominance is a normal shape for legitimate work — story-053's tester was 77% of that run and was correct.

**One consequence follows and is stated rather than left to be discovered: a run can finish above `max_run_cost_usd` by at most one execution allowance.** The run ceiling is a gate checked before an invocation, not a hard cap on the total. Reducing the handed allowance to the run's remainder would close that gap and would reintroduce the starvation the flat allowance exists to avoid, so it is not done. The overshoot is bounded and known; the starvation would be neither.

**Which of the two a reader is looking at is written into the stop.** A run stopped by the run ceiling says what the run has spent and what the ceiling is, and names the stage it did *not* invoke. A run stopped by an execution allowance names the stage that spent it and both figures, and says the invocation was stopped on its budget rather than failing at its work. Both go through the existing `_escalate`, so the commit, the summary and the resume path are exactly what they are for any other escalation, and both append a **`budget-stopped`** event first — its own kind in `execution-history.schema.json`'s enum, so a reader of `events.log` can tell a budget stop from an escalation about the work without reading the reason's wording.

**A budget stop is decided from the recorded cost, never from the wording of a result the harness does not own**, and it is decided *before* the mechanical-failure checks. An invocation that exhausted its allowance looks exactly like one that died: no artifact, no verdict. Left to the checks below it, it would self-route and spend the allowance again. Asking first whether this invocation's own reported cost reached the allowance it was handed is what keeps it out of that path.

**Zero and absent are different values, not one falsy value serving both.** Every read is `is not None`, in the runner's command build, in the run-ceiling comparison and in the allowance handed down. A declared ceiling of zero is a deliberate refusal to run at all and stops the run before any agent is invoked; a declared nothing compares nothing and hands nothing.

**The live allowance and the recorded total are two facts with two lifetimes, and one name does not serve both.** `RunState.entry_cost_usd` is the *allowance*: what the current entry has spent, the only thing the run ceiling is compared against, and therefore policy. It is one number rather than one per stage, because the run ceiling is the only cumulative comparison the harness makes. `cost.json` at the run root is the *record*: one entry per invocation that reported a cost, naming the stage, the entry, the attempt and the cost, with a total rewritten as the plain sum of the list beside it. Nothing rewrites, repairs or prunes the record, and no routing decision reads it — `state.json` remains the coordinator's only routing source. An invocation that reports no cost adds nothing to either, so an absent entry means the harness was told no cost rather than that the invocation was free.

**A resume zeroes the allowance and leaves the record alone**, which is the same division story-062 established for the other live counters: a counter is policy and is reset by the decision to continue, a record accounts and is never reset at all. The ceiling bounds what an *unattended* execution may spend; a human typing `l5-run` is the authorization to spend another allowance. So a run stopped on the run ceiling resumes and proceeds rather than stopping again on the same spend, while `cost.json` continues from where it stood — it is named by no counter, so `entry_artifacts` leaves it at the run root by construction rather than by an exception. Two things here go beyond what the book states and the harness says so rather than presenting them as the book's: the split between a live allowance and a recorded total is this repository's, where the book has usage tracking as one undivided thing; and the allowance resetting on re-entry is a reading of the book's silence rather than of its text.

**A run that stopped on a cost ceiling is exempt from the unchanged-since-escalation guard**, at the guard's call site rather than inside it. That guard refuses a resume when the story, the branch and the harness are all establishably unchanged — which is exactly the state a budget stop leaves the run in, and nothing needs to have changed for the resume to be useful. Exempting at the call site keeps the guard itself saying only what it establishes. `RunState.stopped_on_cost` is what records the case, and the resume clears it with the allowance it accompanies.

**Both ceilings are validated at pre-flight**, by `cost_ceiling_problems(workflow)` beside `retry_routing_problems` and `self_route_problems` and refused through the same `refuse` path: exit 1, one message per problem naming the workflow or the stage and the offending value, no run directory, no `state.json`, no branch and no agent invoked. A declared ceiling must be a non-negative number and must not be a bool — `isinstance(True, int)` holds, so a bare number test would read a declaration of `true` as a ceiling of one dollar.

**The shipped values, and the reason strings that carry their derivation.** `workflows/story-workflow.json` declares `max_run_cost_usd` 90, and `max_execution_cost_usd` 30 on the implementer, 75 on the tester, 10 on the documenter and 30 on the verifier. Each carries a sibling `_reason` string in the shape `max_self_routes_reason` established, **read by nothing**, stating the observed distribution it was derived from. The strings state the figures rather than gesturing at them, because the harness logs they were computed from are gitignored, reach no clone, and are therefore not citable authority — the reason string is the only place the derivation survives into the repository. The principle behind all five, which is what a later reader needs in order to tighten them correctly, is stated there too: *a cap that would have fired on work we were glad to have is set too low*. These are the first ceilings this deployment has run under, they fail by truncating an agent mid-work, and there was no `cost.json` corpus when they were set — so they are pitched at clearly pathological rather than merely unusual, and are to be tightened later against the record this story starts writing.

**The run total is reported where a developer meets the run's end.** `completion-report.md` gains a `## Cost` section and the escalation summary's Recommended Investigation states the same total beside the accumulated attempts, both read from `cost.json` rather than from the entry-scoped allowance, and both facts every run has rather than branches on a reason's text. A resume appends a note reporting the recorded total, the entry's own spend before the reset, and — when the run stopped on a ceiling — that resuming is the decision to fund another allowance.

## A minor, correct finding has somewhere to go

A verifier that noticed something real but too small to fail a run recorded it in `unverified` and the run completed. **Nothing read that array**: no routing decision touched it, no stage received it, and the observation shipped uncorrected. `.harness/stories/story-055.yaml` records the sightings that motivated it: stale numerals that shipped that way, each caught by the verifier that saw it, each correctly classified as non-blocking, none ever fixed. What was missing was not detection — the detection worked unaided — but a destination.

**A passing verdict may now carry `correctable_findings`, and presence is the signal.** On a passing verdict, *after the clean-clone check has passed*, the coordinator resolves the categories those findings name against the verifier stage's own `retry_routing` table, re-enters the workflow at the **earliest destination in workflow order** among them, and runs through to verification again. Earliest rather than one pass apiece: a verdict naming `validation` and `documentation` together enters at the tester and reaches the documenter on the way back, so two categories cost one pass. The field is read only on a passing verdict, because a failed verdict already routes and a correction travels in the retry guidance it already writes.

**It is a retry's shape minus everything a retry spends.** No `retry_count` increment, no `attempts/attempt-N/` archive, no `retry-history.json` entry, no attempt number moving in any rendered prompt filename. The verdict that routed it is unchanged — the archived `verification/iteration-N.json` still records `passed` — and the guidance in force is **cleared** at the routing, for the reason the clean-clone reroute clears it: the passing verdict that routed the pass wrote no guidance, and a stale one read as in force would subject the next verification to a check about an attempt it is not judging. That is the same two-budget separation the self-route keeps, arrived at from the other side: a self-route has no verdict behind it, and a correction pass has a *passing* one.

**The budget is one pass per run, and the bound is what makes termination a property rather than a hope.** `RunState.correction_pass_count` defaults to `0`, so an existing `state.json` still loads, and it is saved and restored with the rest of the state so a resumed run cannot spend the pass a second time. It is **cumulative rather than live**, which is where it differs from `self_route_count`: that count is scoped by the current stage and zeroed at every entry, and zeroing this one would be the cycle it exists to prevent. A second passing verdict still carrying findings is not an error and routes nowhere — the findings are named in a `correction-pass-recorded` event, where a developer meets them, and the run completes with no third invocation of any stage. That is what stops a fix the verifier keeps rejecting from cycling.

**An undeclared category escalates rather than being absorbed.** A finding naming a category the workflow does not define gets the same strictness an unrecognised `retry_target` already gets, and for the same reason: a silent default destination is precisely the drift the routing table exists to remove. The escalation names the offending categories *and* the categories the workflow does define, so it is actionable without opening the definition. It sits **above the budget comparison**, on the reasoning that puts the two unroutable-retry escalations above the ceiling — a verdict that cannot be routed is a bug in what the verifier produced, and that is the reason a developer should read rather than the budget. It goes through `_escalate`, so `retry_count` is untouched, and sits above `archive_attempt`, so nothing is archived.

**The mechanism is turned on by a declaration and off by deleting it.** Removing `correction_pass` from the workflow disables the whole thing with no change to `orchestration/story_coordinator.py`: the same verdict completes the run as it did before this story existed. **No stage name and no retry category is written in orchestration source for it.** The destinations come off the stage's `retry_routing` table through `correction_destination`, the artifact name off the declaration through `correction_pass_declaration`, and the budget off the declaration too — the same property `retry_routing`, `may_not_create` and `clean_clone` already hold, and a search over the coordinator finding none of those names is what holds it.

**The record is an artifact on disk, not only a prompt string**, so a run directory can say why a stage ran again after a verification that passed. `correction_pass_result_file` keys the declared name by the pass number — `correction-pass.json` becomes `correction-pass-1.json` — for the reason the self-route records are keyed by stage, attempt and try: the budget is one today, and a name that silently becomes wrong the moment a workflow declares a larger one is the failure that keying prevents. The coordinator reads the record **back off the file** to inject it, exactly as it does the self-route evidence, so what the prompt says and what the run directory records cannot differ. `correction_pass_statement` composes what the stage is told, and says outright that the coordinator wrote it, that no retry was spent, that the verdict stands, and that the pass corrects words and never behaviour.

**The pass names no retry category and no retry destination.** `routed_category` and `routed_stage` are *cleared* at the routing rather than set to the destination, because a correction pass is not a retry: what the stage is told about why it is running comes off the record, not off the retry placeholders. This is the same reasoning that keeps the self-route out of `retry-history.json`.

**The constraint the whole mechanism rests on is that a correction changes words and not behaviour**, and it is stated in three places a reader can meet it: the schema description a verifier reads when deciding what may go in the field, the prompt paragraph beside it, and the coordinator's own statement to the stage receiving the pass. Nothing a pass changes may alter what any test asserts about the system, and the suite must pass unchanged across a pass.

**A scanner was tried before this was filed and is deliberately not the answer.** Per `.harness/stories/story-055.yaml`, a heuristic over `tests/` reported five candidates of which four were false positives — a check with that false-positive rate trains a reader to skim past it. Nothing here reads prose as language; the detection stays the verifier's unaided judgement, and this story only gives the judgement somewhere to land. It also changes nothing about what makes a finding blocking: the classification in the sightings that motivated it was correct, and this acts on findings that are correctly non-blocking.

**The companion half is the rule, not the mechanism.** `prompts/prose-layer.md` — described under "Prompts" above — carries the redundancy rule that removes the defect class at the source rather than routing it after the fact. The two landed together because a destination for a defect and a habit that stops producing it are answers to different halves of the same question.

## Work a stage cannot own

story-031 escalated seventeen minutes into its implementer on a conflict that was fully decidable before the run started. Its plan named a file under a restricted prefix in `likely_file_changes` with the stage the workflow restricts from creating there, and declared no grant lifting it. The implementer changed exactly the one file the plan named; the harness refused the result correctly. Both halves of the conflict are literals — one in the story artifact, one in the workflow definition — and nothing about it depends on what any agent would later write.

**story-029 made the same assignment and passed, and it was luckier rather than more correct.** Its change was destructive, so reverting it broke seventeen other modules and the revert check read the edits as forced. An additive change reverts cleanly by construction, so there is no version of story-031's work that check could ever have permitted. The revert check is the right instrument for "was this edit forced" and the wrong one for "should this stage have been asked at all"; story-032 moves the second question to plan time and leaves the revert check's semantics untouched.

**Three parts, and they are separable.**

- **A plan-time refusal.** `plan_validation.assignment_problems` reports the conflict, and `l5-plan` leaves the artifact uncommitted in the working tree with the problem printed — the established refusal shape, through the same `refuse_bad_story`. The message names the file, the stage, the prefix in the workflow's own words, and **both** ways out: assign the file to a stage that may own it, or declare a `stage_exceptions` grant naming it. Naming both is what makes it repairable in one exchange while the session's developer is still there, which is the whole reason a plan-time check is worth more than a pre-flight one here.
- **Prefix subtraction became path exemption.** A grant used to be enforced by *removing* the granted prefix from the stage's `may_not_create` list, which can only ever be all-or-nothing. The enforced list is now passed whole and the grants ride beside it as an exemption, so a grant can be finer than a prefix. `grant_covers(granted, path)` is the single decision behind it and the three readers — the plan-time check, the ownership check, the revert check — all call it, so a grant cannot mean one thing when the plan is written and another when it runs. A granted value ending in a slash covers everything beneath it; **any other value covers exactly and only itself**, deliberately not a prefix match, which is what makes a path-level grant exempt one path and leave the rest of the prefix governed by both checks. One `stage-exception-applied` event still fires per grant, carrying the granted value whatever its granularity — and it now fires for *every* grant, where before it fired only for a grant that matched the enforced list verbatim.
- **The standing-test-module convention, in `prompts/planner.md` and here.** A standing test module is enforcement infrastructure: it outlives the stories touching it and exists to hold later work to a rule. A story whose deliverable *is* such a module is not an exception to independence — it is ordinary work for the stage that owns validation — so it is assigned to that stage in `likely_file_changes` and needs no grant at all. That is the common case, and making it the cheap one is what keeps the refusal rare. **Its cost:** the deliverable lands one stage later than the story's main change, so the stage writing the code cannot run the new module itself. **Two things bound it:** nothing enforces it — it is guidance in a prompt and a paragraph here — and what actually holds is the refusal, which is deterministic and names the alternative.

**Both halves of the check are read off the loaded workflow**, through `stage_restrictions`, per the standing rule: `orchestration/plan_validation.py` names no stage and no path prefix, docstring included, exactly as the strictness check beside it already promised.

**The check is plan-time only and is deliberately absent from `l5-run`'s pre-flight**, the same reasoning `strictness_problems` carries: pre-flight refusal would make committed artifacts that have already run unrunnable. That is not hypothetical — run over the committed corpus under `.harness/stories/`, the new check reports story-029's artifact (fifteen entries assigning files under the restricted prefix to the restricted stage) along with story-007, story-013, story-014, story-019, story-020 and story-021. (After story-042's narrowing the set is story-029 — thirteen entries survive, all naming modules story-038 renamed away — with story-007, story-013, story-014, story-019 and story-021; story-041's is reported by nothing.) story-057 widens it again, since existence no longer exempts anything; which artifacts fall in it is held by the corpus assertions in `tests/test_plan_assignment_refusal.py` rather than by a number written here. **story-029 being refused by the new check is the intended outcome, not a regression**: its plan really did assign that work to a stage that could not own it, and it passed on a property of its own diff rather than on the assignment being sound. Pre-flight is untouched, so none of those artifacts becomes unrunnable.

**story-042 narrowed the check to the rule it enforces.** As written the condition guessed: an entry says only which stage is expected to *write* a file, never whether writing it means creating it, and the restriction is about creation alone. So the check refused both cases, including the one the restriction does not cover. Modifying a file that already exists is legal and governed by a different instrument entirely — the revert check, which restores the stage's edits under governed prefixes, re-runs the suite, and decides whether they were forced. The plan-time check was applying the creation rule to a case the revert check owns. The evidence was story-041's planning session, refused for four entries naming files that all exist here; not one could have been created, and the story required editing every one. The three stories before it had their implementers edit files under the same prefix, all three were permitted by the revert check, and none of the three plans predicted those edits — so the check refused a plan that forecast its forced edits and passed a plan that stayed quiet. **A check that rewards a vaguer plan is the defect**, not the refusals themselves. That reading held until story-056 showed what the narrowing left behind; the paragraphs below carry the current rule.

The added conjunct was one question: `(root / entry_path).exists()` is false. `exists()` rather than `is_file()`, so a path present as a directory counts as existing exactly as a regular file does. The grant check still short-circuits above it, so `grant_covers` keeps deciding grants and the existence question decides nothing about them.

**story-057 replaced that exemption with a grant requirement, and the exemption is what it cost.** What story-042 left behind was a plan the harness accepts and a run the harness can only refuse. story-056 assigned `tests/conftest.py` to the stage that writes the implementation; the work was right and the plan was legal, and the run escalated twenty-five minutes in — because reverting an implementation change never breaks the suite. The assertion that would hold the new behaviour belongs to a later stage and has not been written yet, and a comment-only change is refused by the same arithmetic. So between the two run-time checks there is no version of an ungranted governed entry a run can accept: the ownership check refuses the created file outright, and the revert check refuses the modification unless reverting it breaks the suite. The escalation was structurally guaranteed, so it carried no information about the work.

**The reconciliation already existed and nothing required it.** A granted path is skipped by `governed_edits` exactly as it is skipped by the ownership check, so a plan that assigns the file *and grants it* worked before this story as it does after — story-056's own artifact was repaired by adding two grants and resuming. All story-057 adds is requiring the plan to say so. story-042's permission stands: a plan may still assign a file the target already holds to the stage restricted beneath that prefix. The grant is what makes the plan say it completely.

**Existence words the message; it no longer decides the verdict.** An entry offends on the stage-and-prefix match alone, with the grant short-circuit unmoved above it, and the root is consulted only once an entry has already been decided to offend. An absent file describes a creation the ownership check refuses; a present one describes a modification the revert check governs and, for an implementation or comment-only change, can only refuse. Both wordings end with **the same resolution clause**, built once in the module, so a plan repaired at either fault is repaired the same way: reassign the file to a stage that may own it, or declare a `stage_exceptions` grant naming that file for that stage — whose `reason` field is required. The required reason is stated in the reported problem because not knowing the field exists at all is the failure mode story-056 hit.
