**The judgement sits at plan time because it is a question about intent.** What has to be answered is *is this file implementation or test logic for this story*, and a human answers it where a human is present. It is deliberately not moved into the revert check, which would have to read a diff as language to answer it and would put a target stack back into harness source that story-040 and story-041 removed. Neither run-time check changes: the ownership check and the revert check decide exactly what they decided before, on the same inputs, with the same records and the same events.

**Existence is resolved against the target root**, the third parameter both `assignment_problems` and `artifact_problems` now require. Not the harness root and not the process working directory: the three coincide in this repository and will not in general, and no parameter carries a default, so a two-argument call raises rather than silently answering against whatever directory the process is standing in. `scripts/l5-plan` passes the root `main` already resolves through `harness_config.find_target_root` — one argument through `report()`, which prints and returns exactly what it did before. The fact is derived from the repository rather than declared by a new `likely_file_changes` field: a field would be the planner's claim about something the filesystem already knows, the check would have to verify it against the repository anyway to be trustworthy, and the forty-odd committed artifacts do not carry it.

**The check is still a prediction and does not claim otherwise.** A file present when the plan is written may be gone by the time the story runs — which since story-057 can only change which of the two faults the message named, never whether the entry was reported. The run-time ownership check and the revert check remain the authority on what a stage actually did; this one only refuses a plan that could not have worked out.

**No run is affected.** `assignment_problems` is called only from `artifact_problems`, which is called only from `scripts/l5-plan`. `run_story` calls neither, at pre-flight or anywhere else, before this story or after it — and a test asserts that rather than leaving it to inspection. A change to a check usually does move run behaviour; this one does not, which is why it is written down — and it held through story-057 as it held through story-042. The five plan-time classes stay plan-time only, so no artifact already committed becomes unrunnable by either change. After the narrowing the corpus was not silently empty either: story-029 is still reported, because story-038 renamed away every module its thirteen entries named, so none of them exists — the corpus keeps a true positive, and after story-057 it keeps it under the other wording as well.

**This story's own plan was not governed by the check it adds**, for the eighth time in this pattern's history, and the cause is a new one worth naming: the artifact was written and committed by an `l5-plan` session that ran before `assignment_problems` existed. It happens to be clean — it assigns its test module to the stage that owns validation, which is the convention this story writes down — but it was not checked. Enforcement begins with the next plan, which is one step earlier than the stale-workflow and stale-import cases, since the artifact rather than the run is what escapes.

## A workflow declaration may reference configuration

Since story-046 a workflow definition may name a value the target's own `.harness/config.yaml` supplies, written as `{{key}}` and **only as a whole list entry**. Exactly one declaration does: the implementer's `may_not_create` is `["{{tests_dir}}"]`.

**The resolution happens once, when the definition loads.** `harness_config.load_workflow(harness_root, name, config)` walks the parsed definition through `_resolve_tokens` and returns a dict in which the token is already the configured string, so `stage_restrictions` and its six readers — the ownership check, the revert check's governed edits, the stage-baseline capture, `stage_exception_problems`, and both of `plan_validation`'s checks — read exactly what they read when the declaration was a literal, and not one of them gained a config parameter or a resolution step. For this repository the loaded definition is identical in value to the one that shipped before, which is why "this repository's behaviour is unchanged" is a checkable claim rather than an intention.

**`config` is a required positional, not a defaulted one.** A caller that omitted it would load a definition with the configured entries silently missing — that is, with the create restriction gone — which is a quieter wrong answer than a `TypeError`, and the restriction disappearing silently is precisely the failure this mechanism must not introduce. Both callers pass the config they had already loaded: `story_coordinator.run_story` and `scripts/l5-plan`.

**An unset key resolves the entry *out of the list*, and never to an empty string.** A restriction whose prefix is `""` is a prefix every path is under, the exact opposite of what the absence means. A target that declares no `tests_dir` therefore has an implementer with no create restriction at all — `stage_restrictions` reports no pair for it — which is what a Go project, or any target with no single test directory, is. The rule is stated in the key's own schema description as well as here.

**What is referable is a one-entry mapping, and that is the narrowness.** `harness_config.workflow_token_values(config)` returns `{"tests_dir": config.get("tests_dir")}`, written as a literal read per key rather than a lookup by whatever name the definition carries — so the keys this resolution reads are visible to a reader and to `test_config_keys_are_obeyed`'s AST scan exactly like every other configured key. A general mechanism letting any declaration reach any config key was considered and rejected: one key needs this, and the narrow token leaves every existing reader of a loaded workflow untouched. `{{branch_prefix}}` in a workflow is refused like any other unanswerable reference.

**An unanswerable reference refuses the run at pre-flight.** `load_workflow` raises `UnresolvedWorkflowToken`, carrying `problems` in the shape every pre-flight refusal enumerates, and `run_story` catches it and prints through `_refuse_unresolved_workflow_token` and the shared `refuse()`: the header names the workflow, each problem names the token and lists what a declaration may reference. It sits where the workflow load already sat — after the undeclared-config-key refusal and above the run directory, `state.json`, the log, the branch and any agent invocation — so a refused run creates nothing. A token-shaped string anywhere a list entry cannot be dropped from is collected as unresolved too, rather than silently substituted, because there is nowhere else an unset value could go.

**The prompt renders the same value rather than restating it.** `context_assembler.config_context` maps `tests_dir` beside `allowed_tools`, and `build_context` passes `{**config, "allowed_tools": allowed_tools}` — the grants keep arriving as their own optional argument so a call that omits them renders what it always rendered, while every other configured fact comes off the config the caller already passed. `prompts/tester.md` renders `{{tests_dir}}`, so changing the config changes the rendered prompt with no prompt edit. Its baseline-resolution sentence was rewritten in the same move to say *use the shared baseline resolution the existing validation already provides* rather than naming the file this repository happens to keep it in — a prompt states what the tester should do, not this repository's filename.

**Both shipped configs declare it explicitly.** `.harness/config.yaml` and `templates/config.yaml` each set `tests_dir: tests/` with a comment saying what its absence means, so a newly initialized target states its test location rather than inheriting one.

## No target-stack literal in harness source

The harness runs against any repository. Its only tie to a target's language, toolchain or directory layout belongs in `.harness/`, which is that target's own configuration. An audit on 2026-08-15 found five ties outside it: a Python snippet executed as a version probe and a configuration key whose name is a language, both in `orchestration/story_coordinator.py`; the `may_not_create` line of `workflows/story-workflow.json`; and two lines of prose in `prompts/tester.md` naming a pytest layout.

**The rule is a scan, not a paragraph, and the choice has a record.** Two rules of exactly this shape were written down here before: the `git diff HEAD` baseline rule was documented *and* injected into every stage prompt and shipped five more times, and the git-history-loader rule was documented and shipped three more times. Both stopped when a scan landed. Two of the five ties also sit in a prompt, which no fixture reaches — `prompts/tester.md` is prose an agent reads — so "every configurable value is proven configurable" could vary every key in existence and still not notice a pytest filename sitting in it. Only a reader or a scan sees that, and readers had missed it since the file was written.

**Where the two halves live.** The declaration and the scan are `orchestration/harness_source.py` (above); the judgement is `tests/test_no_target_stack_in_harness_source.py`. The module reports mentions and judges none of them — the test module holds **two allowlists**, keyed by repository-relative path and the exact text of the matched line rather than by line number, so an unrelated edit above a tie does not churn the list and read as burn-down. `TEMPORARY_TIES` (**empty since story-046**; 3 entries after story-041, 36 before it) holds the mentions that name or assume a target's stack or layout. `PERMANENT_MENTIONS` (8 entries since story-043, 9 before it) holds the mentions that are the opposite of a tie — a docstring saying every scalar `story_parser` produces is a Python `str` is a fact about this code, and the shebang each `scripts/l5-*` entry point carries names the interpreter *this* harness runs under — and **every entry carries a one-line reason**, so the permanent half is a judgement on the record rather than a suppression list. The ninth entry was `orchestration/harness_config.py`, whose `RETIRED_CONFIG_KEYS` spelled a retired key; story-043 deleted the mapping, and the allowlist entry had to go with it or the two-way set equality would fail. Only two entries name a file under `orchestration/` now, both docstrings about this code: `story_parser.py`'s type-coercion note and `story_coordinator.py`'s `self_route_problems`. The two lists together equal exactly what the scan reports against this repository — since story-046 that is `PERMANENT_MENTIONS` alone, 8 pairs — asserted in both directions, and every reported entry asserted to land on **exactly one** of them. Disjointness used to be its own assertion, intersecting the two lists directly; with `TEMPORARY_TIES` empty that intersection has one possible answer, so story-046 replaced it with the partition — a question this repository can still fail, since an entry on neither list is a new tie. (Before story-046 it was 11 distinct `(path, line text)` pairs from 12 findings, `prompts/tester.md:47` matching both rules.) The classification rule sits in the module docstring beside the declaration so the next one is not re-argued.

**Why they are two lists and not one.** `TEMPORARY_TIES` reaching empty was the completion signal for `.harness/requests/the-interpreter-is-not-assumed-to-be-python.md` and `.harness/requests/the-test-location-comes-from-configuration.md` — the two stories queued behind story-040, which fix the ties it grandfathers. Merge the two lists and the signal is gone — a list that stops shrinking cannot be told from work that finished. The signal has now fired; the split stays, because the next tie a scan finds goes in one list or the other and the same question has to be answerable then.

**story-040 fixes none of the five**, deliberately. `orchestration/story_coordinator.py`, `workflows/story-workflow.json` and `prompts/tester.md` carry no edit on its branch, and no run-time behaviour changed.

**The burn-down has since run once, and it removed exactly its own entries.** story-041 took off every `orchestration/story_coordinator.py` entry — the version probe, the record's interpreter-shaped fields, and the retired configuration key with the prose explaining it — together with every entry in `schemas/clean-clone-result.schema.json`, `schemas/revert-check-result.schema.json` and `schemas/harness-config.schema.json`. **What remained temporary after it was the two `prompts/tester.md` lines and the `workflows/story-workflow.json` restriction, and nothing else** — the completion signal for `the-test-location-comes-from-configuration` alone, which story-046 then fired. The coordinator came off that module's untouched tuple and both of its entries came off `AUDITED_TIES` — asking the scan for a tie by name only works while the tie is there. In their place `REPAIRED_SCHEMAS` and `test_the_repaired_schemas_carry_no_mention_at_all` assert the scan reports *nothing* in the three schemas, and `test_the_coordinator_carries_no_temporary_tie` asserts the same for the coordinator in the weaker form the surviving permanent mention allows. Both read the claim off the scan rather than off the diff, so an entry removed while its mention is still in source goes red. One mention does survive in the coordinator, and deliberately: `self_route_problems`'s docstring naming the language *this harness* is written in, which the classification rule calls the opposite of a tie.

**The burn-down is finished.** story-046 removed the last three temporary entries — the workflow's create restriction, now the `{{tests_dir}}` token resolved from configuration when the definition loads, and the two lines of `prompts/tester.md` prose, which now render the configured location and describe the shared baseline resolution without naming the file this repository keeps it in. `harness_source.scan()` over this repository reports exactly the eight `PERMANENT_MENTIONS` and nothing else, which is story-040's completion signal for the whole burn-down. All five ties the 2026-08-15 audit found are repaired.

**An empty list is the shape an assertion goes vacuous against, and no assertion in that module rests on reading it.** Emptying `TEMPORARY_TIES` took six assertions vacuous, in two waves, and every one was replaced rather than deleted or skipped.

The first wave is the ones that *iterated* the list. `AUDITED_TIES` and the story-diff pair are gone; in their place `REPAIRED_FILES` and `test_the_repaired_files_carry_no_mention_at_all` assert the scan reports **nothing at all** in `workflows/story-workflow.json` and `prompts/tester.md`, and `test_the_burn_down_is_complete` asserts the scan reports nothing outside `PERMANENT_MENTIONS` — both read off the scan, not off the list. The control is `HISTORICAL_TIES`: the three exact lines that used to sit in those files, kept verbatim and replanted one at a time into a throwaway copy, where the same scan must report each and the same two-directional comparison must call it unexpected. Without that, "the scan reports nothing there" would be satisfied just as happily by a scan that had stopped reading the file. The stale-entry control moved onto a permanent entry, since no temporary one is left to remove.

The second wave is subtler and was caught only in verification: three assertions still *named* the empty list to decide something — `reported & set(TEMPORARY_TIES) == set()`, `entry not in TEMPORARY_TIES`, and a whole test asserting the two lists disjoint. None iterates it, so none reads as a loop over nothing; each is simply a comparison against the empty set, which cannot go red. Two sat beside a live `PERMANENT_MENTIONS` assertion, so their tests survived and only the lines were dead — the shape that is hardest to see, because the test still fails when the behaviour breaks and the reader credits the wrong assertion for it. The replacement is `classification_problems(findings, temporary=None, permanent=None)`: exclusivity and coverage asked as one question, whether the two lists classify each reported entry **exactly once**. An entry on neither is a new tie, which this repository can still produce, so the question stays answerable with one list empty; and because the lists are parameters, a control hands the same check an entry sitting on both and observes it object. `test_the_coordinator_carries_no_temporary_tie` and `test_a_legitimate_mention_is_permanent_and_never_a_tie` now call it in place of their dead lines, each still carrying its own replant control.

**The lesson worth keeping is the detection rule**, since the next story to empty a burn-down list meets the same thing: a list going empty makes every assertion that *mentions* it suspect, not just every loop over it, and the check is whether the assertion can still fail for some state of the repository rather than whether it still reads like an assertion. The repair is to re-ask the question against something that can still be non-empty — here, what the scan reports — and if a question is genuinely answered elsewhere, to say so in the module where the assertion used to be, so a later reader does not read the removal as a weakening.

**What the scan does not catch, stated where a reader meets it.** `STACK_TOKENS` is a guess about languages nobody has tried and is incomplete by construction — a Ruby `Gemfile` or an Elixir `mix.exs` is reported by nothing here, which the suite demonstrates by planting both and finding them invisible. It is worth having anyway because it catches the *shape* of the mistake, which recurs, rather than every instance of it. The layout half **cannot read `orchestration/` at all**: this repository's own suite is called `tests/`, so in Python source a `tests/` literal cannot be told from an honest reference to it, and the half therefore reads only the target-facing files where a path can only mean a target's. `.harness/` and `tests/` are outside the scan entirely. The module is exempt from its own scan **by name and by nothing else** — the token list would otherwise report itself — and a second file carrying the same tokens is reported. And the scan is not tamper-proof: deleting it alongside a forced repair is not caught here at any granularity, and no assertion in the module implies otherwise. Every one of those limits is asserted by reading the module's text rather than by trusting it to be there.

**The matcher's boundary is "not a letter or a digit", not `\b`.** That is what makes `clean_clone_python` and `platform.python_version()` visible — `_` and `.` are not alphanumeric — while `pipeline` and `pipe` are not, because `e` is; `\b` inverts both. The alternation is longest-token-first so `python3` claims its own match rather than being read as `python` followed by a digit, which the boundary would then reject and hide entirely. The layout pattern keeps the left boundary and drops the right one, because each path already ends in a slash and requiring a non-alphanumeric after it would hide `tests/conftest.py` — the exact shape being looked for. All four constructed cases are run through `scan` rather than reasoned about.

**Every absence assertion is paired with a demonstration it can fail.** A tie planted in a file that is clean today is reported and turns the equality assertion red; a list entry left behind after its tie is removed turns it red the other way; the identical text that is invisible under `.harness/`, under `tests/` and in `orchestration/` is reported when planted in `prompts/`; the exemption is paired with a twin file carrying the module's full text, which *is* reported. All of it runs against a throwaway root rather than by editing this repository, in the style `tests/test_schema_inventory_location.py` established.

## A test builds the workflow it needs, or says why it reads the shipped one

The suite's standing rule is that a test never writes a stage name, a path prefix or an artifact name inline; it derives them from the workflow. The rule stays in force. What story-048 changed is **which** workflow a test derives them from.

**Satisfying the rule by reading `workflows/story-workflow.json` turned deployment decisions into facts the suite enforces.** Two dozen modules under `tests/` resolve the shipped definition, and almost none of them is testing that definition: they are testing a *mechanism* — does the coordinator self-route, route a retry, refuse a malformed declaration, enforce a boundary — and reached for the live artifact only to avoid spelling a name. The cost was measured rather than predicted. story-047 granted one stage a `max_self_routes` budget, a correct one-line deployment change, and reddened four assertions in `tests/test_self_routing_retry.py`, a module with nothing to say about whether that grant was right. How many stages this deployment budgets had become something the suite asserted.

**The question is subject versus input, and it is decided module by module.** Is what this repository ships the *subject* of this assertion, or an input to it? An input reader builds the workflow it needs. A subject reader — the retry-ceiling search over this repository, the schema inventory against `schemas/manifest.json`, the plan-time checks that read a real story against the real workflow — keeps reading what is deployed and carries a recorded reason saying why. `prompts/tester.md` asks the question since story-047. It cannot be answered by grep: the scan below reports both kinds identically, and a story that classifies by pattern converts the assertions that were right.

**The builder lives in `tests/conftest.py` and is compositional rather than canonical.** `workflow_stage(...)` returns one stage declaration carrying exactly what the caller asked for — an argument not passed produces no key, so a stage built with no `max_self_routes` declares no budget, which the coordinator treats differently from a declared zero; `max_self_routes` is therefore checked against a sentinel rather than against `None`, since `0` and `False` are values a test builds deliberately. `build_workflow(*stages, name=..., escalation_rules=...)` assembles them, assigning positional names unless the caller named one. A route names its destination with `StageRef(index)`, by position, which the builder substitutes: the caller points at a stage rather than writing the name the builder was about to choose, so the derive-names rule holds from the caller's side too. There is deliberately **no single fixture** shared by thirty modules — one would accrete until it was a second shipped workflow with the same coupling — and a key more than one module needs becomes an argument here rather than being spelled at each call site (`**extra` covers the one-off).

**A built definition is meant to be run, not only inspected.** `materialize_workflow(workflow, root, ...)` writes it where a run finds it and writes a prompt template per stage, because the shipped templates are named for the shipped stages. `rules/` and `schemas/` are the *harness's* and are symlinked at this repository's unless the caller supplies otherwise — converting away from the shipped workflow is not thereby building a rule set. Directories a caller asks for by `copy=` are **copied, never linked**, because an entry point under `scripts/` resolves its own harness root with `Path(__file__).resolve()`, and `resolve()` follows a symlink straight back here, which would load the shipped workflow and undo the point. The prompt fields a materialized template renders are named once in `BUILT_PROMPT_FIELDS`, so a module asserting on a rendered prompt derives the field from the fixture rather than from what this repository's templates happen to say; `built_stage_prompt` omits the retry-guidance field for the verifying stage, mirroring the harness's own division — guidance is written by the judge and read by the stage the retry routes to.

**`configured_workflow` is the seam.** The shared `target_root` fixture writes the target's `.harness/config.yaml` from it, defaulted to `"story-workflow"` so an unconverted module configures what it always did. A converted module overrides that fixture with its built definition's name and overrides `harness_root` with a materialized one, and the same fixture then drives a real run under a definition this repository does not ship — the same code path, a different workflow. The harness's own fixed artifact names (`changed-files.json`, `test-results.json`, `verification-result.json` and the rest) are constants in `conftest.py` beside `VERIFYING_STAGE`: `context_assembler.build_context` and `story_coordinator` read those off the run directory by fixed name regardless of what a workflow declares, so they are facts about the harness rather than about this deployment, and a built workflow that must reach those code paths calls its artifacts these.

**The live-artifact scan now reads two routes to the same read.** The original matches on the *shape of the path* — a join onto one of the module-level names standing for this repository, reaching one of the five artifact families, in both the `/` and `.joinpath(...)` idioms. story-048 added the **workflow resolvers**: `conftest.shipped_workflow` and `harness_config.load_workflow`, handed one of those repository-root names or handed nothing at all, since a defaulted root reaches the same file and leaving it out would make the widening evadable by deleting an argument. Matched on the callee name alone, qualified or bare, for the reason `_is_subprocess_call` gives: requiring a particular spelling makes the check evadable by an import. `_is_a_repository_root_name` is deliberately narrower than `_names_the_repository_root` and not a copy of it — an argument must *be* the name, because `Path(REPO_ROOT).parent / "elsewhere"` names the root and does not resolve to it. Those two helpers join the path *inside* themselves, in a module the path-shape route is not reading while it reads this one, so before the widening the idiom the suite actually writes was the invisible one. Widening it against the unconverted tree took the report from 20 modules to 24: four were resolving the very same artifact through the helper and reporting to nothing. Over the converted tree it reports 21, one of which — `test_stage_tool_grants.py` — no other route reaches.

**The scan's stated limits survive the widening and are still asserted true of it.** It still follows nothing across a module boundary — `harness_config.load_rules(REPO_ROOT)` and `context_assembler.load_template(...)` resolve live artifacts and are not reported, because only the two *workflow* resolvers are named. It still cannot tell a subject from an input; every report is a place to ask the question. It still does not evaluate expressions, so a root the module computed is invisible. And it is not tamper-proof. The boundary limit has a consequence the scan cannot state: a module that borrows a helper from a module being converted changes with it, which is why `tests/test_defective_retry_guidance.py` and `tests/test_unfinishable_retry_verdict.py` were repaired alongside `tests/test_retry_routing.py` and `tests/test_self_routing_retry.py` with nothing reporting them.

**`GRANDFATHERED_LIVE_ARTIFACT_READERS` became `DECLARED_LIVE_ARTIFACT_READERS`, a mapping from module name to reason.** A mapping rather than a tuple so a name cannot sit there without an answer to the subject-versus-input question, and a companion test asserts every entry states one. It is still asserted **equal** to what the scan reports in both directions: a module that starts reading a live artifact fails because it is not declared, and a module converted off the set fails because the list still names it. A subset assertion either way would let one of those pass silently. The widening and the conversion had to land in one story precisely because they move that list in opposite directions.

**`tests/test_shipped_workflow_is_valid.py` is where an assertion about this deployment goes.** It holds the shipped definition to the three validators the coordinator runs at pre-flight — `self_route_problems`, `retry_routing_problems`, `stage_exception_problems` — each asserted clean *and* shown to report a definition that violates it, because "the shipped workflow has no problems" passes just as happily against a validator that has stopped looking; the violating definitions come from the builder, never from a mutated copy of the shipped one, so a control there is a statement about the validator rather than about today's deployment. It also states what this project *intends* of its definition (which stages, in which order, which restriction, which budgets), which is where an assertion that goes red on a workflow change has done its job. Every configuration assertion displaced from a converted module moved here rather than being deleted, named with the module it came from — a built workflow says nothing about the deployment, so leaving one behind would have made it vacuous. That the builder resolves nothing this repository ships is proved by *running* it with every route to the filesystem closed, rather than by scanning its source for the absence of a read. And the property the whole conversion exists for is demonstrated here rather than argued: `test_a_workflow_change_moves_this_module_and_not_a_mechanism_module` mutates a built harness root the way a deployment change would and shows a converted module unmoved by it while this module reports it — the shape of story-047's `max_self_routes` grant, run as a test instead of discovered as a failure.

**The conversion is complete, and what remains declared is not a remainder.** Twenty-one modules under `tests/` had their workflow reads converted to a workflow they build, and the two that borrow their helpers — `test_defective_retry_guidance.py` and `test_unfinishable_retry_verdict.py` — were repaired with them. `DECLARED_LIVE_ARTIFACT_READERS` holds 21 names and the scan reports exactly those 21, both differences empty. **A name on that list is not necessarily a module that reads the shipped *workflow*.** The scan reports a resolution of any of the five live artifact families, so an entry can survive its workflow read being converted: `test_clean_clone_check.py`, `test_escalation_summary.py`, `test_retry_history.py`, `test_revert_baseline.py`, `test_stage_baseline.py` and others remain declared for the schema this repository ships for the record under test or the retry ceiling in `rules/execution-rules.json`, and their reasons say so in those words. Reading a reason as "this module still reads the shipped workflow" will mislead; read it for which artifact it names.

**A module can be an input reader and a subject reader at once, and `tests/test_documenter_before_verification.py` is the worked example.** It was first declared whole, on the reasoning that its subject is the order *this* deployment declares; verification rejected that, correctly — a module leaving the input-reader set without being converted is exactly the escape the story forbids, and the mechanism half was still there to convert. It was then split rather than reclassified. Its runs are driven by two definitions it builds, one in today's order and one in the previous order, each materialized into its own harness root, so the module can show the *move* — that the documenter now runs before the verifier and did not before — by running both, which no read of a single deployed definition can do. What stays behind is named assertion by assertion in the declared list, not module by module: three that read this deployment's routing table, which is story-045's acceptance criterion, and three that read `prompts/verifier.md`, the template this repository ships. **The declared reason is the right granularity when a module is mixed** — "this module is a subject reader" would have covered the mechanism half back up. The statement of today's stage order moved to `tests/test_shipped_workflow_is_valid.py` as `test_this_deployment_documents_before_it_verifies`.

**The runtime is two measurements, both of `.venv/bin/python -m pytest tests/ -q` on the same machine.** Before: 526.45 s over 2537 tests. After: 445.08 s over 2584 tests — 81 s faster, about 15%, while collecting 47 more tests, because a fixture with the two or three stages an assertion needs runs a real run faster than the shipped four-stage definition does. Recovering that was a side effect rather than the point, but it is the number a later story should expect to move if the builder grows a default that materializes more than a test asked for.

## A test resolves this repository's own history only when that history is its subject

The subject-versus-input question above is asked of the files this repository *ships*. story-052 asks it of the commit graph those files sit in, and lands the mechanism that reports it. `tests/test_baseline_honesty.py` gains a **seventh rule** — the story plan calls it the sixth, counting the rules as they stood before story-047's live-artifact rule was written; the module numbers it seventh because it is.

**The commit graph could not have been a sixth family of the live-artifact scan, and the reason is structural rather than a matter of effort.** That scan matches a *path shape* joined onto one of the module-level names standing for this repository. A history read has no path to match: it is a call, to one of the five shared helpers in `tests/conftest.py` — `story_commit_range`, `story_diff`, `repository_file_at`, `function_source_at`, `revision_carrying` — whose repository argument is defaulted or spelled. So `history_reads(source, module, resolvers=None)` is a **call-site scan**, and the two rules share neither a predicate nor a vocabulary, which `test_this_rule_draws_nothing_from_the_six_above` asserts the way the live-artifact rule asserts its own independence: by walking the call graph reachable from the scan and by asserting each other rule's word list disjoint from `HISTORY_RESOLVERS`. `_names_the_repository_root` is the one deliberate share and predates both.

**The helpers are the sanctioned route to the history, and this rule is about who takes it.** The third rule exists so that no module writes a *second* route; nothing here reverses that, and `tests/conftest.py` keeps its helpers' behaviour and signatures unchanged. What the seventh rule adds is the question of whether the history is the assertion's subject or its instrument. `HISTORY_RESOLVERS` maps each helper to the position its `repo` argument may be written at — `story_commit_range` takes one positionally, the other four keyword-only, recorded as `None` — so a call resolved against a repository the test *built* goes unreported while a defaulted call is reported, because every one of the five defaults to the root the harness lives in and skipping the defaulted form would make the rule evadable by deleting an argument. Three spellings are recognised — imported by name, reached as an attribute of the module holding it, and bound to a local alias — and the alias half reuses `_imported_names`, the one place in the module mapping a local name back to what it imports, rather than a second copy.

**The list is declared and classified; story-052 converted nothing.** `DECLARED_HISTORY_READERS` is a literal mapping — **30 module names** when story-052 wrote it, **four** after story-053 — asserted equal in both directions to what the scan reports over the test directory. **Four are subject readers**, each carrying a stated reason naming what about this repository its assertions are about: `test_baseline_honesty.py` (its regression set is committed evidence — the known vacuous assertions recovered at the bound of the story that carried each), `test_baseline_resolution_is_single.py` (a value defined exactly once across the tree, and a declaration that moved in a named commit), `test_git_history_loading_retired.py` (a retired practice absent at an endpoint and present at a baseline), `test_validation_module_naming.py` (this repository's own module names and their declared origins). The other **26 were `PENDING`**, which is a statement that the module has not been decided yet rather than a verdict that its read is wrong — story-053 converted all 26, and the paragraphs after the next three record how. The reasons live on the list entries rather than in thirty module docstrings, precisely so story-052 edited no module it was not converting: its only test-directory change was `tests/test_baseline_honesty.py` itself.

**`PENDING_CEILING` is the part that makes the next instance go red rather than being found two stories later.** It was `26` at story-052 and is `0` now. A converting story lowers it; nothing raises it. A module that starts resolving this history must be converted or argued to be a subject reader, and neither adds to the count. It is a literal integer compared against a length inside `within_the_ceiling`, and that is asserted **of the function's body** rather than claimed in prose — the module runs its own scans over the source of the comparison, because a rule against reading the commit graph that read the commit graph to enforce itself would be the third instance of the practice it exists to stop. A control constructs a pending list one entry longer and observes it rejected, rather than resting on the live assertion continuing to pass.

**A module leaves the pending class by being converted, never by being reclassified.** That is the constraint the conversion story inherited, and the 26 pending entries were its work list. `tests/test_documented_claim_support.py` was on it deliberately: the read story-051 spent its whole retry budget on — a pinned revision rebased away by a squash-merge, then a content search that collided with the story's own documentation quoting the figure it searched for — is the case this rule exists to have caught. The test immediately above it asserts the same behaviour against a sentence constructed in the test, with no git at all, and asserts more besides. **Guidance was not what closed this**: the rule had been documented and shipped past three times before a scan landed, which is the same shape recorded for the `git diff HEAD` baseline rule and the git-history loader above.

**Stated limits, and they are the usual ones.** The scan follows nothing across an assignment or a module boundary, so a module's own helper wrapping one of the five is invisible; git reached by hand through `subprocess` is the first and third rules' business; `getattr` and an assembled repository argument are not seen; and it cannot tell a subject from an instrument, which is the central limit — every report is a place to ask the question, and the classification beside each name is a human answer rather than something the scan derived. Reading a file the test wrote is not a history read and is not reported, which is the point rather than a gap, since that is the shape a converted module takes. The limits paragraph is registered in the module's parametrized limits test alongside the six rules already there, so it goes stale red rather than quietly.

**`prompts/tester.md` now asks the question of the commit graph too**, beside the shipped-artifact half story-047 wrote. It names the five helpers, says outright that they are the sanctioned route, draws the subject/instrument line, and — when the history is an input — names the idiom rather than describing a principle: the `target_root` fixture already builds a repository under a temporary directory, runs `git init` in it and commits what it wrote, with `commit_setup` for committing what a test adds afterwards. Extend those rather than write a fourth beside them. It closes one step earlier still: check whether the assertion needs a history at all.

Two constraints govern how that guidance is written and asserted, and both are worth keeping. **The prompt names identifiers, never the module or directory they live in** — `prompts/` is inside the target-facing half of `harness_source.scan()`, so a `tests/` path or a stack token in that template would be reported as a target-stack tie; `target_root` and `commit_setup` are identifiers and are not. And **the guidance is asserted from the rendering, not the template**: `context_assembler.load_template` + `render` against the fixture's harness root, with the assertion that the template carries `{{` and the rendering does not, so a phrase found was found in what a stage is handed. The template is wrapped to a column, so a multi-word phrase is broken across lines at a position nothing about the guidance decides; `flattened()` normalises whitespace before searching, and a later assertion on rendered prose should do the same. Every positive check there is paired with the same rendering cut down to remove the commit-graph paragraphs, where the phrase must be absent.

## Every history-reading test builds the history it needs

story-053 is the converting story the rule above was built to make possible. All **26 pending modules** were converted; `DECLARED_HISTORY_READERS` now holds the same **four subject readers** with the same recorded reasons, and `PENDING_CEILING = 0`. **Zero is a ceiling reached, not a class retired** — the scan, the `PENDING` vocabulary, the five planted-violation controls, the "What it does not cover" paragraph and the ceiling machinery all survive unchanged, so the next module to take a history dependency is reported by the scan, absent from the declared list, and red at the ceiling.

**The conversion was decided call site by call site, and the deciding question was what the read was fetching.** Three answers, and the helper called did not determine which:

- **An input** — a sentence, an earlier version of a function, the text of a file before a repair — is *built*: either as a history the test constructs under a temporary directory, or as the text carried in the tree as a committed fixture.
- **A frozen past fact the harness enforces at run time** — the `story_diff` scope assertions, each re-stating that some past story touched nothing outside its scope — is *restated over a constructed repository in which the predicate is shown both to pass when the scope was respected and to report when it was not*. Before, such an assertion recalled a fact; now it checks one, and it carries its own negative control.
- **Genuinely about this repository** stays, and stays declared. That case did not arise here; the four subject readers are the ones story-052 already identified.

What no assertion did was change what it claims. The conversion moves where a test's evidence comes from. Only one test name was removed across the thirty edited modules (below), and every other surviving name in every converted module is the name it carried before.

**A converted scope predicate takes the repository it asks about as a parameter, and that retired an idiom.** Modules that previously wrote `_unchanged_by_this_story(rel)` against the defaulted root now write `_unchanged_by_this_story(rel, repo)` and are called twice — once against a constructed story that respected the path, once against one that violated it. The knock-on is worth keeping: `tests/test_baseline_resolution_is_single.py` and `tests/test_shared_baseline_resolution.py` used to point a module under test at a synthetic repository by `monkeypatch`ing the `story_diff` name it had imported. With the repository an argument, that redirect is gone from both — the module's own predicate runs unmodified and nothing reaches inside it, which is a stronger statement of what the redirect was there to establish. Both modules are subject readers and were edited for this reason alone; neither changed class.

**Two builders and one reader landed in `tests/conftest.py`, beside the five resolvers whose behaviour and signatures they did not touch.**

- `constructed_story(tmp_path, *, respected=(), violated=(), violation="modify", validation_rel=..., name=...)` builds a repository in which one story has already run and committed: commit 1 is the pre-story state, commit 2 is the story's own run commit, which adds the validation file, makes an unrelated change it is entitled to, and touches exactly the `violated` paths. That is the shape of a finished branch — the shape in which `git diff HEAD` reports nothing whatever the story did — which is why the assertion must be bounded at both ends. `violation` selects `modify`, `delete` or `add`, the third being the case a `--diff-filter=MD` assertion deliberately lets through. `constructed_story_range` and `constructed_story_diff` are the one-call spellings, so a converted assertion keeps its own predicate's shape and only the repository moves. The builder generalises the shape `tests/test_shared_baseline_resolution.py`'s pre-story control family already established rather than inventing a fourth beside `target_root` and `commit_setup`.
- `build_history(root, commits)` builds a repository commit by commit, returning each sha, for a module proving a resolver against a *shape* — renames applied first, then writes, then deletions, so one commit can move a path and rewrite it at its new name. `squash_onto(root, base)` replays everything since `base` as one commit, which is the shape that makes a pinned sha unreachable.
- `history_fixture(name)` reads a text this repository once carried out of `tests/history-fixtures/`. It raises rather than returning empty when a fixture has moved, so a stale reference fails as itself rather than as an assertion that has quietly stopped seeing anything.

**`tests/history-fixtures/` is where a past text is kept, and its own README states the convention.** Seventy files plus that README. Names are `<subject>.at-story-<NNN>-<baseline|endpoint>.<ext>.txt`, the two bounds being exactly what `story_commit_range` resolves; a handful predate that shape and the README names them. The **`.py.txt` suffix and the separate directory are load-bearing**: the suite's scans glob `tests/*.py`, so a fixture is neither collected as a test module nor reported as carrying the very defect a pre-repair fixture exists to carry. A fixture is committed evidence — in the tree, diffable, and changed visibly — where `git show <revision>:<path>` answers the same question only until the revision is rebased away, the path renamed or the branch squashed.

**`tests/test_shared_baseline_resolution.py` stopped resolving out of this repository and gained the coverage it lacked.** It proves the resolution, so it went first and everything else leans on it. Two additions:

- `RESOLVER_SIGNATURES` spells out all five resolvers' full signatures and compares them against `inspect.signature`, with the `HARNESS_ROOT` default folded back to its name so the literal is not machine-specific. A parameter appearing, disappearing, changing default or moving between positional and keyword-only reports a different string. The constraint that the conversion changed neither behaviour nor interface is therefore asserted rather than claimed, and a control shows the comparison can differ.
- `repository_file_at`, `function_source_at` and `revision_carrying` are far less proven than `story_commit_range` is, and are now proved against constructed histories over the four shapes this repository has actually hit: a **rename**, a **revert-and-restore**, a **squash**, and a **path with no history**. The rename cases (one through `repository_file_at`, one through `function_source_at`), the squash case and the one-line `revision_carrying` case each carry a planted break and are shown answering *differently* under it — the hobbled helper returns the wrong text, or silently answers HEAD where the real one raises. The revert-and-restore and no-history cases rest instead on their own positive `pytest.raises` assertions: a helper that degraded to the working tree would return text where they require a refusal, and they would fail. Only the first four demonstrate a difference under a break; the block at the end of the no-history case applies the `repository_file_at` break but the hobbled helper still raises there, so that block shows nothing the assertions above it do not.

**Two pieces were named by hand.** `tests/test_documented_claim_support.py::test_story_049s_own_sentence_is_reported_when_a_run_adds_it` was **retired rather than converted** — the sole test name removed by this story. It was a weaker duplicate of the test immediately above it, which asserts the same behaviour against a sentence constructed in the test with no git at all and asserts more besides. Its one genuine contribution, that real prose is messier than a synthetic one-liner, was taken by making that test's `ADDED_CLAIM` a multi-sentence constant with the messiness of real prose and requiring every line of it to reach the report. And `tests/test_validation_module_naming.py`'s merged-module origin rule gained an **exclusion**: a range call that names its own `repo` is not that rule's business. A constructed repository holds exactly one story, so there is no lineage to disambiguate and naming an origin the constructed file never declared makes the resolution *raise* rather than answer — asserted, not argued. The rule still bites where it did: a call resolving a range out of the repository the helpers default to still has two possible answers in a merged module and still has to say which one it means. Because the conversion left neither merged module with an unqualified call of its own to point at, the companion and the negative control now append `QUALIFIED_RANGE_CALL` to the module's real source, so what is scanned is still that module.

**Emptying the pending class forced two edits inside the rule itself, both of which strengthen it.** `test_every_entry_is_classified_and_every_subject_states_its_reason` asserted both classes non-empty; it now asserts `pending_entries() == []` while keeping the non-emptiness requirement on the subject class, because a scan reporting nothing at all would otherwise satisfy the two-way equality for the wrong reason. And `test_the_read_story_051_spent_its_retry_budget_on_is_listed_as_pending` inverted: that module is no longer reported by the scan and is on neither class, which is the stronger statement, and the test carries a control planting a history read into the same module's source to show the scan has not stopped reporting.

**What still depends on this repository's commit graph, and it is a short list.** The four subject readers, by design — their subject *is* this history, and they correctly refuse to answer in a truncated clone. And one read the scan does not cover and this story neither created nor converted: `tests/test_clean_clone_check.py::test_the_commits_describing_the_cause_as_unestablished_still_say_so` searches commit *messages* through a hand-written `subprocess` call, which is the first and third rules' business rather than this one's, and which the "What it does not cover" paragraph names exactly. **`.github/workflows/tests.yml` still carries `fetch-depth: 0`**, and its comment still explains it by the differential tests in a module it calls `test_story_011_validation.py` — a name story-038 renamed, carried today by `tests/test_execution_history.py`, and a reason this story removed. `.github/` was outside this story's scope, so the checkout depth and its comment are left for a later one to decide; the remaining dependency is now the four subject readers and that commit-message search, not the differential family.

## A check that re-runs the suite announces itself before it starts

Every stage says it has started before it works. The checks that run *between* stages did not: they emitted their result and nothing else. The clean-clone check re-runs the whole suite in a fresh clone, so the console sat with `verification passed` as its last line for the length of a suite run — `.harness/stories/story-058.yaml` describes those gaps on main from story-050 to story-055 as intervals of roughly eight to nine minutes, the only ones of their kind in those runs. It reads as a hang.

**The rule is general rather than one missing event: a check that re-runs the configured test command says so before doing so.** Today that is the clean-clone check, the revert check and, since story-066, the suite run a stage declares; a check added later gets it by calling the same helper. The third one is the evidence that the general rule was worth stating: it was written by calling `_suite_rerun_started` with a phrase of its own, and nothing else about announcing had to be decided again. The revert check was equally silent and looked fine only because it sits between the implementer's started and completed lines, so a reader already had a line on screen saying work was in progress — **quiet hidden by position rather than by design**, and the silence would have reappeared somewhere new the moment the check moved.

**The announcement is an event, not a print.** `append_event` stays the coordinator's one write path, for the reason its own docstring gives: a second output path, however correct, is drift waiting to happen. An event is also what makes the wait visible in `l5-status` and in the run's structured history rather than only on the console of whoever started the run. `events.log`'s frozen line format is unchanged — one bracketed timestamp, one prose line.

**One helper, one spelling of the kind.** `_suite_rerun_started(run_dir, stage_name, artifact, phrase)` sits beside the existing result-event helpers and is the only function in `orchestration/story_coordinator.py` that names the `suite-rerun-started` kind, the way `append_event` is already the only writer of the history files. It writes **no stage name and no artifact name** of its own: both are parameters taken from the declaration the caller already read, so each announcement names the result artifact its own check writes. `phrase` is the whole of what distinguishes the two messages — a fresh clone with the story committed for one, the reverted paths and what the check decides for the other.

**The announcement is made inside the check, not at the call site**, and that placement is what makes the two inseparable: removing the `clean_clone` declaration silences the announcement and the result together, with no change to orchestration, and the same holds for `revert_check`. Both check functions gained a **required, keyword-only** `stage_name` for the reason `capture_stage_baseline`'s `accounted_for` is keyword-only — a defaulted argument would let a later call site silence the announcement by omission, which is exactly the failure the placement closes. `revert_check` announces in the branch that actually builds a clone, after the baseline resolves; the no-baseline path announces nothing, because it runs no suite and so has nothing to announce.

**Nothing about the checks themselves changed** — what they run, when they run, what they revert, what they commit and what their results mean are all as they were. There is no progress output during a check: no percentage, no live tail of the suite's output, and nothing that makes a check faster or concurrent with anything. Since story-066 there is something afterwards instead: every one of these runs writes its whole combined output to a file in the run directory and names the path in its own result — see the next section.

**The schema gap this exposed is the one worth remembering.** `schemas/execution-history.schema.json`'s closed `event` enum gained `suite-rerun-started` and also `revert-check-permitted` — a kind the coordinator has emitted since story-017 and the enum never declared. The gap was invisible only because no schema-validating fixture run had governed edits, so no history under test ever carried the kind. A coordinator-written artifact whose schema the coordinator does not validate at run time (see the `schemas/` bullets above) is discovered by a fixture that happens to exercise it, or not at all; the standing answer is the enum-completeness assertion in `tests/test_execution_history.py`, which holds every kind the coordinator can pass to `append_event` against the kinds the schema declares, so a future kind added without its schema entry is reported rather than waited for.

**The ripple was repaired by deriving, not by restating.** The modules that pin a clean run's whole event stream as an exact equality now drop the announcements **by kind, never by wording**, and assert positionally that each announcement stands immediately before an event of the same stage — so a spurious or misplaced announcement still fails, and whole-stream equality over the stage events is untouched. Typing the announcement's text into those literals would have been a second spelling of a message composed inside the check, needing a retype every time another check learns to announce itself. This is the standing lesson under a new key: a test that pins the event stream should derive its expectation from the workflow or from the coordinator's behaviour rather than restate a longer literal sequence or count.

## The coordinator runs the suite a stage declares

The tester used to run the target's whole suite inside its own turn. On this repository that command outlasts the agent tool's per-call ceiling, so it is forced into the background, the stage polls, and the turn ends on a result that only arrives if the turn continues. `.harness/stories/story-066.yaml` records the arithmetic behind that: the suite takes roughly eleven minutes, `clean-clone-result.json` measured it at 667.00s, and story-065 escalated at the tester three times this way, spending $11.95 and producing nothing. **It is arithmetic rather than misbehaviour** — no prompt about piping, backgrounding or polling can make a command complete inside a call shorter than the command, which is why the guidance `prompts/tester.md` carried about exactly that was deleted rather than sharpened.

**The other whole-suite runs in a cycle are coordinator subprocesses, and neither had ever failed this way, because a subprocess cannot end its turn.** So the repair is not a better instruction but a third subprocess. The runs a cycle now makes, and who makes each, are:

| Run | Who runs it | What it decides |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The revert check | Coordinator subprocess, in a clone with the stage's governed edits reverted | Whether the stage's modifications were permitted |
| The declared suite run | Coordinator subprocess, in the target's own working tree, after the declaring stage's turn ends | Whether the suite passes where the stage left the tree |
| The clean-clone check | Coordinator subprocess, in a fresh clone with the story committed | Whether the work stands up outside the developer's tree |

story-066 **moved** one run from an agent to the coordinator and removed none, so the count of whole-suite runs in a cycle is what it was. What changed is who owns the process.

**The stage authors and the coordinator executes.** `prompts/tester.md` now says the stage authors validation, records what it wrote, and executes no test command; the coordinator runs the configured `test_command` after the turn ends and reads its exit status. Zero advances the workflow to the next stage with no further invocation of the declaring stage, so the passing case costs one invocation. Non-zero brings the stage back in place through the existing `self_route` decision, on the budget it already declares — which is why this needed no new budget, no new counter and no change to the tester's declared `max_self_routes`.

**Why the structured counts are gone rather than moved.** `test-results.json` used to carry `status`, `tests_run`, `tests_passed`, `tests_failed` and `failures`; it now requires `tests_written` and defines nothing else. Those fields existed only because the stage parsed its own suite output, and **any contract requiring them is a contract on the target's test framework** — the assumption story-040 and story-041 took out of harness source. Moving that parsing into a prompt would not be removing it, so they were removed rather than reproduced. What replaces the verdict is stronger than what it replaces: an exit code from a subprocess the coordinator owns rather than an agent's assertion about a run it made itself. The reasoning is written in the schema's own `description`, where a reader meets the artifact and would otherwise read the narrowing as a gap, and the constraint that keeps it true is that **no module under `orchestration/` derives a count of tests from a suite run's output** — the output is stored and passed on as text.

**What the coordinator's record carries.** `suite-run-result.json` holds `ran`, `command`, `runner`, `exit_code`, `output_tail` and `output_path`, with optional fields expressed by absence as `clean-clone-result` already does. It carries no count of tests run, passed or failed and no list of failing names. `CleanCloneResult` is the shape it reuses — the same fact in all three cases, a command, the executable it ran under, an exit status, a tail and a path.

**One departure from the plan is worth reading, because it was observed rather than argued.** The check does *not* substitute `verification_runner` for the command's first word. That key exists so the clean-clone check can exercise an environment other than the one the developer works in and find an incompatibility before CI does; this run is *in* the tree the developer works in — it is the run the stage used to make for itself, made where the stage would have made it — so the configured command runs exactly as written and `runner` is its own first word. With the substitution in place, `tests/test_config_keys_are_obeyed.py` escalated its fixture runs at the tester, because that module deliberately configures a `verification_runner` that does not resolve. The reasoning is recorded at the function and in the schema's `command` description, not only here.

**Every one of the three runs now keeps its whole output, not only a tail.** A self-routed stage has to diagnose a failure from the record, and a tail loses a failure that happened early in a long run; reading a file costs nothing and cannot time out, which is why this closes that gap without reintroducing a command an agent must survive. `suite_output_file(artifact)` derives the output filename from the declared result artifact name in **one place**, and all three sites use it, so no output filename is spelled at any call site. `_write_suite_output(destination, output)` is the one function that writes the file and reports where it went, returning `None` when the caller named no destination — the optional-by-absence convention the surrounding records use. `run_clean_clone` gained an `output_path` parameter defaulting to writing nothing, so a caller that names none records none, and the revert check and the clean-clone check are otherwise unchanged in what they run, where they run it and what they decide. **The tail stays in every record beside the new path**, so each artifact remains readable on its own.

**The failure route is a self-route and not a retry**, and it inherits every property a self-route already has rather than restating any: `retry_count` and `max_retries` are untouched and unread, no `attempts/attempt-N/` directory is written, nothing is appended to `retry-history.json`, and the attempt number does not move. `SUITE_FAILED` joins the failure constants and the `self-route-result` schema's enum, and it belongs beside `defective-retry-guidance` rather than among the mechanical three for the same reason that one does — it is a fact computed from what the stage produced, not a judgement about the work, and no verifier saw it. `self_route_statement` gained a branch naming both the result artifact and the path to the full output, and telling the re-running stage to read the output file rather than reach for a suite it no longer runs. An exhausted budget escalates with the reason `self_route` already returns, through no second escalation path.

**The mechanism is enabled by a declaration and by nothing else.** A workflow that declares `suite_run` nowhere runs the declaring stage exactly as this repository ran it before: no suite is run by the coordinator, no result artifact is written, no announcement is emitted and no self-route is taken on that account. That is the property the fixture workflows in `tests/` hold, and it is why `tests/test_config_keys_are_obeyed.py`'s fixture harness — which configures a `test_command` that deliberately does not exist — strips this check along with the other suite-executing ones.

**The story that adds this check is not protected by it.** A coordinator process imports `orchestration/story_coordinator.py` once, when it starts, so the process driving the run that *adds* a check is the process that predates it: the declaration lands on the tester stage, and the coordinator executing that stage never reads it. For story-066 this meant the stage stopped running the suite — the prompt rewrite takes effect immediately, being read per turn — while nothing had yet started running it in the stage's place, so the run had no whole-suite result between the implementer's turn and the verifier's. The gap is one run wide and closes by itself on the next one, but it is the general shape of any story that moves work from a prompt into orchestration, and a plan should expect the verifier rather than the coordinator to be where that story's own suite regressions surface. It is also why the story asked for validation that drives `run_story` against a temporary target with the agent runner faked: that evidence does not depend on the running process having loaded the code under test.

**No new event kind.** The check announces itself through the existing `suite-rerun-started` kind and emits no result event of its own on a pass, so `execution-history.schema.json`'s closed enum needed no addition and the modules pinning a clean run's whole event stream stayed green untouched — they already drop announcements by kind and hold each to standing immediately before an event of the same stage. A passing suite makes no routing decision the advance was not already going to make, and the record on disk says it ran.

## Run directory anatomy

    .harness/runs/story-001/
      state.json                current stage, status, retry_count,
                                self_route_count (live only, scoped by the
                                current stage), correction_pass_count
                                (cumulative for the run, since the budget is
                                per run), guidance_in_force (the entries
                                of the retry guidance directing the attempt now
                                running; empty when none is), resume_count
                                (which entry of the run is executing; zero
                                before any resume), entry_cost_usd (the live
                                cost allowance — what this entry has spent,
                                and the only thing the run ceiling is
                                compared against), stopped_on_cost, branch,
                                and the three resume fields: story_digest,
                                escalation_commit, harness_revision
      events.log                append-only stage/retry/escalation events
      execution-history.json    the same events, structured; one entry per log line
      implementation-summary.md
      changed-files.json        implementer's record (modified/created/deleted)
      tester-changed-files.json tester's record, same schema definition; required tester output
      test-results.json
      documentation-report.md   documenter's account of what it wrote and why
      documenter-changed-files.json
                                documenter's record, same schema definition;
                                required documenter output, and since story-045
                                read by the verifier that runs after it
      verification/iteration-1.json
      retry-guidance.json       written by the verifier on failure
      clean-clone-result.json   the clean-clone check's record, coordinator-written
      revert-check-result.json  the revert check's record, coordinator-written;
                                absent when the stage's record named no governed path
      suite-run-result.json     the record of the suite the coordinator ran after
                                the declaring stage's turn ended, coordinator-
                                written; absent when no stage declares a
                                suite_run
      <suite-result>-output.txt one per coordinator suite run, holding that run's
                                whole combined stdout and stderr; named from the
                                run's own result artifact by suite_output_file,
                                so the clean-clone, revert and declared runs each
                                have their own beside their record
      claim-support-result.json the claim-support check's record, coordinator-written;
                                absent when no stage declares the check, and
                                recording ran false with a reason when it could
                                not run
      self-route-<stage>-attempt-N-try-M.json
                                one per self-route, coordinator-written; absent
                                from a run in which no stage failed mechanically
      correction-pass-N.json    one per correction pass, coordinator-written,
                                keyed by the pass number; absent from a run
                                whose passing verdict carried no correctable
                                finding, and from every run under a workflow
                                that declares no correction pass
      prompt-<stage>-attempt-N.md          the first invocation's rendered prompt
      prompt-<stage>-attempt-N-try-M.md    a self-route's, so the first survives
      stage-baseline/           what the tree held under a stage's governed prefixes
        implementer/            when that stage first ran; file copies, one dir per
                                stage, merged first-seen-wins across attempts
      attempts/attempt-1/       superseded attempt's artifacts, canonical filenames
      resumes/resume-1/         the entry a resume ended: everything above whose
                                name is keyed by retry_count or by
                                verification_iterations, each at the position it
                                held beneath the run directory — the prompts,
                                the self-route records, verification/iteration-
                                *.json and attempts/. Absent from a run never
                                resumed
      retry-history.json        one entry per retry taken; absent when none was
      cost.json                 one entry per invocation that reported a cost —
                                stage, entry, attempt, cost — and their total;
                                coordinator-written, never reset, and left at
                                the root by a re-entry. Absent when no
                                invocation reported a cost
      completion-report.md      or escalation-summary.md

The files at the root always describe the *current* attempt of the *current* entry, and `resumes/resume-K/` holds the entries that ended — see "Resuming a run" above. The record is the exception on both counts: `retry-history.json`, `cost.json`, `execution-history.json`, `events.log`, `state.json` and the terminal report account for the whole run, are never keyed by a counter, and never move. `attempts/attempt-N/` appears only once a retry has occurred: before each retry begins, the coordinator copies the superseded attempt's stage artifacts there under their canonical filenames (`changed-files.json`, `implementation-summary.md`, `test-results.json`, `tester-changed-files.json`, `documentation-report.md`, `documenter-changed-files.json`, `verification-result.json`, `retry-guidance.json`). The documenter's two are new to the archive since story-045 and were added by nothing: `archivable_artifacts` derives the list from the loaded workflow, and the reorder simply means the documenter has run by the time an attempt is superseded, so its artifacts are there to copy. A run that never retries has no `attempts/` directory at all, so its absence is itself evidence. N is the same attempt number the rendered prompts use, so `prompt-implementer-attempt-1.md` and `attempts/attempt-1/` describe one attempt. **A self-route leaves that correspondence alone**: it writes no `attempts/` directory, does not move N, and adds a `-try-M` suffix to its own prompt only, so `prompt-implementer-attempt-1.md` remains the prompt the first invocation of attempt 1 was given and `prompt-implementer-attempt-1-try-1.md` is the re-run's. Reading a stage's self-routes off the directory is therefore a matter of the `self-route-*` records and the try-suffixed prompts beside them, and their absence is evidence in the same way `attempts/`'s is. **A correction pass leaves it alone on the same terms and one step further**: it writes no `attempts/` directory, appends nothing to `retry-history.json`, does not move N, and does not suffix the prompts — a pass re-enters the workflow at a stage whose attempt number has not changed, so its rendered prompts land under the ordinary `prompt-<stage>-attempt-N.md` names. What says a pass happened is the `correction-pass-N.json` record, the two events in the stream, and `correction_pass_count` in `state.json`. **The cost of not suffixing them is that the re-entered stages' first prompts are overwritten in the run directory**, which is the same known cost `clean-clone-result.json`'s absence from the archive carries and is recorded here for the same reason: the pass number is not a try number and the attempt has not moved, so a suffix would have to be a third key. The evidence lost is a prompt the pass supersedes rather than one it builds on — the opposite of the self-route case, where the failed invocation's prompt is exactly what the re-run needs — and widening the naming is the fix if it ever costs a debugging session.

`execution-history.json` is deliberately *not* among the archived artifacts: it is not a stage output, so `archivable_artifacts` never names it and a retry neither copies nor overwrites it. It stays one continuous stream across every attempt and every entry of the run — a resume neither moves it nor rewrites it — which is what lets a retried, escalated or resumed run be reconstructed from it end to end.

Three artifacts describe a retry and none of them substitutes for another. `retry-guidance.json` looks *forward*: the verifier writes it for the next attempt, saying what to fix. `attempts/attempt-N/` is the *evidence*: the actual artifacts that attempt produced. `retry-history.json` is the backward-looking *record*: one entry per retry actually taken, naming the attempt that ended, the blocking issues that failed it as the verifier recorded them (field for field, never a prose summary), the stage execution was rerouted to, the `attempts/attempt-N/` directory holding that attempt's evidence — a path a later resume moves under `resumes/resume-K/`, resolved by the event the resume appends rather than by editing the record, which is never rewritten — and the guidance that attempt's failure produced — optional, because a retry can be routed without any being written. Its named consumers are the documenter, the assist agent during recovery, and the adjudicator later; they read it directly rather than filtering the full event stream. Overlap with `execution-history.json`, which records each retry decision as one event among all events, is expected rather than a defect: that artifact is the chronological stream, this one is the retry-scoped record. Since story-024 `escalation-summary.md` renders it too, through this same reader — see "The escalation summary" above.

`clean-clone-result.json` is absent from the archive for a different reason, and it is a known cost rather than a decision. `archivable_artifacts` reads the three places a stage names artifacts — `outputs`, `changed_files`, and the keys of `schemas` — and the clean-clone declaration is none of those, so a second attempt's record overwrites the first's. The event stream still carries that a first clean-clone failure happened; only its captured output is lost. Widening the declaration is the fix if that ever costs a debugging session. `revert-check-result.json` is absent from the archive for exactly the same reason and on the same terms — a known cost carried deliberately, not an oversight — though it bites less: the revert check escalates rather than retrying, so a run that writes the record twice is a run that was permitted the first time. `claim-support-result.json` is the third on those terms, and it bites less again: the record describes the text the *current* attempt added to the document, which is the only text the verifier now judging it is being asked about. `suite-run-result.json` and the `-output.txt` files beside all three records are on those terms too, and the declared suite run is where it bites hardest, because that record is overwritten by every self-route as well as by every retry: what survives is the failure the stage is currently working on, and the event stream carries that the earlier ones happened. Widening the declaration is the fix if that ever costs a debugging session.

`stage-baseline/` is the odd one in this directory, and it is worth saying exactly what it is and is not. It holds **file copies, not JSON**: one directory per **stage**, `<stage>/`, mirroring repository-relative paths, holding the content of every file under that stage's declared `may_not_create` prefixes as it stood when that stage *first* ran. It carries no attempt component, and since story-037 no attempt number is derived for it anywhere — the directory answers "what did this stage find", a question the run has one answer to however many attempts it takes. Both the directory name and the stage it is captured for come off the workflow declaration; `stage_baseline_dir(run_dir, baseline, stage_name)` and `capture_stage_baseline(run_dir, target_root, baseline, stage_name, prefixes, *, accounted_for)` name no stage and no prefix.

Four properties of it are load-bearing:

- **The file set is `git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard`**, the same tracked-plus-untracked set `_build_clone` carries into a clone — not tracked files alone. A file an earlier stage of this run created and never committed (the coordinator commits once, at `_complete`) has no HEAD version, and capturing it is the whole reason this exists.
- **First seen wins, per path.** A capture merges into the stage's directory: a path the baseline already holds keeps the content it was first captured with, and a path new since the last capture is added at its current content. Nothing is ever overwritten. This is what stops a re-entered stage from snapshotting its own completed edits, which would make the revert a no-op, the suite pass, and the check report a permission it never established.
